\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\left(\frac{1}{2}\psi_{a}^{(j,1)}\partial_{t}\psi_{a}^{(j,1)}+\frac{1}{2}\psi_{a}^{(j,2)}\partial_{t}\psi_{a}^{(j,2)}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int\text{d}t\left(\Sigma_{13}\psi^{(L,1)}_{A}\psi^{(L,2)}_{A}+\Sigma_{23}\psi^{(R,1)}_{A}\psi^{(L,2)}_{A}+\Sigma_{14}\psi^{(L,1)}_{A}\psi^{(R,2)}_{A}+\Sigma_{24}\psi^{(R,1)}_{A}\psi^{(R,2)}_{A}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{N}{2}\int\text{d}t\left(\Sigma_{13}G_{13}+\Sigma_{14}G_{14}+\Sigma_{23}G_{23}+\Sigma_{24}G_{24}\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\left[-\frac{2{\cal J}^{2}}{2^{q}}{N\choose q}-\frac{2JN}{q}\left(G_{LL,12}^{q}+G_{RR,12}^{q}\right)+\frac{2JN}{q}\text{i}^{q}\left(G_{LR,12}^{q}+G_{RL,12}^{q}\right)\right]\ .

(427)

Integrating out the fermions with the help of the relation (
382
), shortening the labels according to
(L,1)\to 1
,
(R,1)\to 2
,
(L,2)\to 3
,
(R,2)\to 4
, and using the fact that
\Sigma_{12}=\Sigma_{34}=\Sigma_{LR}
by construction,
we get

\displaystyle\langle\Phi(\sigma)^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\text{d}G_{AB}\text{d}\Sigma_{AB}}{4\pi\text{i}/(NT)}\exp\left\{N\left[\log\left(2\cos\left(\frac{T}{4}\sqrt{(\Sigma_{14}-\Sigma_{23})^{2}+(\Sigma_{13}+\Sigma_{24})^{2}}\right)\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2\cos\left(\frac{T}{4}\sqrt{(\Sigma_{14}+\Sigma_{23})^{2}+(\Sigma_{13}-\Sigma_{24})^{2}+4\Sigma^{2}}\right)\right]-\frac{NT}{2}\sum_{AB}\Sigma_{AB}G_{AB}

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{2{\cal J}^{2}}{2^{q}}T{N\choose q}-\frac{2JNT}{q}\left(G_{13}^{q}+G_{24}^{q}\right)+\frac{2JNT}{q}\text{i}^{q}\left(G_{14}^{q}+G_{23}^{q}\right)\right\}\,,

(428)

where again we have focused on the time-independent saddles.

8.4.1
Exact computation

We can first evaluate the integral explicitly. The calculation is similar to the one of
\langle|z|^{4}\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\Phi(\sigma)^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{4JNT}{q2^{q}}}\left(e^{\frac{\text{i}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{+}}{N}}+e^{-\frac{\text{i}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{+}}{N}}+e^{\frac{\text{i}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{-}}{N}}+e^{-\frac{\text{i}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{-}}{N}}\right)^{N}e^{\frac{2NTJ}{q}\sum s_{AB}G_{AB}^{q}}\,\Big|_{G_{AB}=0}\,

(429)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{4JNT}{q2^{q}}}\sum_{0\leq n_{i}\leq N,\sum_{i}n_{i}=N}e^{\frac{\text{i}(n_{1}-n_{2})}{N}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{+}}e^{\frac{\text{i}(n_{3}-n_{4})}{N}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{-}}e^{\frac{2NTJ}{q}\sum s_{AB}G_{AB}^{q}}\,\Big|_{G_{AB}=0}\,,

(430)

where
s_{14}=s_{23}=\text{i}^{q},s_{13}=s_{24}=-1
and we have introduced the differential operators

\displaystyle\hat{f^{\prime}}_{+}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\partial_{G_{14}}+\partial_{G_{23}})^{2}+(\partial_{G_{13}}-\partial_{G_{24}})^{2}+4T^{2}\Sigma^{2}},

(431)

\displaystyle\hat{f^{\prime}}_{-}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\partial_{G_{14}}-\partial_{G_{23}})^{2}+(\partial_{G_{13}}+\partial_{G_{24}})^{2}},

(432)

Following a similar calculation as in section (
386
), we get

\displaystyle\langle\Phi(\sigma)^{2}\rangle=e^{-\frac{4JNT}{q2^{q}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{i}\leq N,\\
\sum_{i}n_{i}=N\end{subarray}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k^{+}_{1},k^{+}_{2},k_{3}\leq m,\\
0\leq k^{-}_{1},k^{-}_{2}\leq n\\
\sum k_{1}^{+}+k_{2}^{+}+k_{3}=m\\
\sum k^{-}_{i}=n\end{subarray}}\sum_{l^{+}_{i}=0,l^{-}_{i}=0}^{k_{i}^{+},k_{i}^{-}}\frac{(\frac{\text{i}n_{12}}{N})^{mq}}{(mq)!2^{mq}}\frac{(\frac{\text{i}n_{34}}{N})^{nq}}{(nq)!2^{nq}}

\displaystyle\quad\left(\text{i}^{q}\frac{2NJT}{q}\right)^{(k_{1}^{+}+k_{1}^{-})q}\left(-\frac{2NJT}{q}\right)^{(k_{2}^{+}+k_{2}^{-})q}(2Te^{-\frac{\pi}{q}}\sigma)^{k_{3}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\Delta(k_{i}^{+},k_{i}^{-})\,,

(433)

where
n_{12}=n_{1}-n_{2}
and
n_{34}=n_{3}-n_{4}
. In the large
T
limit, as we show in the exact computation of
\langle z^{4}\rangle
, the leading contributions are the summation of the contribution obtained by keeping only one exponential differential operator in (
429
). The operator
e^{\pm\frac{\text{i}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{-}}{N}}
contributes 1 when
q=4m
and 2 when
q=4m+2
. The result of operator
e^{\pm\frac{\text{i}\hat{f^{\prime}}_{+}}{N}}
will be a monomial of
\sigma
while its expression is not very illuminating so we omit here.

8.4.2
Saddle point computation

We deform the contour so that the integral converge. In the current case, the contours are rotated as

\displaystyle G_{13}=g_{13}\,,\quad G_{24}=g_{24}\,,\quad\Sigma_{13}=i\sigma_{13}\,,\quad\Sigma_{24}=i\sigma_{24}

(434)

and

\displaystyle G_{14}=\text{i}e^{\text{i}\frac{\pi}{q}}g_{14}\,,\quad G_{23}=\text{i}e^{\text{i}\frac{\pi}{q}}g_{23}\,,\quad\Sigma_{14}=e^{-\text{i}\frac{\pi}{q}}\sigma_{14}\,,\quad\Sigma_{23}=e^{-\text{i}\frac{\pi}{q}}\sigma_{23}\,,

(435)

so that the effective action for computing
\langle\Phi(\sigma)^{2}\rangle
is
15
15

15

To get rid of the factor
1/2
we have scaled the variables as
\sigma_{AB}\rightarrow 2\sigma_{AB}
.

\displaystyle S=-\frac{4NTJ}{q2^{q}}-2NT\sum_{ab}\left(\frac{J}{q}g_{ab}^{q}+\frac{\text{i}}{2}\sigma_{ab}g_{ab}\right)+N\log(2\cos(Tf^{\prime}_{+})+2\cos(Tf^{\prime}_{-})),

where we have defined

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}(\sigma_{14}+\sigma_{23})^{2}-(\sigma_{13}-\sigma_{24})^{2}+e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}4\sigma^{2}},

(437)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{-}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}(\sigma_{14}-\sigma_{23})^{2}-(\sigma_{13}+\sigma_{24})^{2}}.

(438)

The equation of motion of
g_{AB}
gives universally

\displaystyle-\text{i}\sigma_{ab}=2Jg_{ab}^{q-1},\quad(ab)=(13),(24),(14),(23)\ .

(439)

As discussed in the previous
\langle|z|^{2}\rangle
computation, the equation of motion of
\sigma_{ab}
depends on the value of time
T
.

Very short time

When time
T
is very short, the cosine function can be approximated by a constant. Then the only saddle point is

\displaystyle g_{ab}=0\,,\qquad\sigma_{ab}=0\ .

(440)

The on-shell action, including a 1-loop determinant
\frac{1}{(TN)^{4}}
, around this saddle point is

\displaystyle\langle\Phi(\sigma)^{2}\rangle=\left(2\cos\left(Te^{-\frac{\text{i}\pi}{q}}\sigma\right)\right)^{N}e^{-\frac{4JTN}{q2^{q}}}=\left(2\cos\left(T\Sigma\right)\right)^{N}e^{-\frac{4JTN}{q2^{q}}}\ .

(441)

The results agree with
\langle\Phi\rangle^{2}
as can be seen from (
420
).
Notice that the trivial saddle (
440
) remains a saddle point for a large range of
T
, and the on-shell action around this saddle point, ie (
441
), is true within this large range.

In summary, we have shown that at very short time the trivial saddles dominate the
\langle\Phi^{2}\rangle
which approximately equals to
\langle\Phi\rangle^{2}
, we conclude that at short time the trivial saddle dominates and
\Phi(\sigma)
is self-averaging.

Short time

When the time is larger, the determinant term in the action cannot be approximated by a constant, and thus we expand it to the second order of
T
.
The saddle point equation for
\sigma_{ab}
is now

\displaystyle\sigma_{13}T=4\text{i}g_{13}\,,\qquad\sigma_{24}T=4\text{i}g_{24}

(442)

\displaystyle\sigma_{14}T=-4\text{i}e^{\frac{2\pi\text{i}}{q}}g_{14}\,,\qquad\sigma_{23}T=-4\text{i}e^{\frac{2\pi\text{i}}{q}}g_{23}\ .

(443)

Combining with (
439
), we get the non-trivial saddle solutions

\displaystyle\frac{2}{JT}=g_{13}^{q-2}=g_{24}^{q-2}=-e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}g_{23}^{q-2}=-e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}g_{14}^{q-2}

(444)

Each solution gives a contribution to
\langle\Phi(\sigma)^{2}\rangle
as

\displaystyle 2^{2N}e^{-\frac{2JTN}{q2^{q-1}}-Ne^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}\sigma^{2}T^{2}/4}e^{4N\frac{q-2}{2q}(JT/2)^{\frac{2}{2-q}}(e^{\frac{4m_{1}\pi\text{i}}{2-q}}+e^{\frac{4m_{2}\pi\text{i}}{2-q}})}e^{-4N\frac{q-2}{2q}(JT/2)^{\frac{2}{2-q}}(e^{\frac{4m_{3}\pi\text{i}}{2-q}}+e^{\frac{4m_{4}\pi\text{i}}{2-q}})}\,,

(445)

where
m_{i}
are integers.
It is cumbersome in the following to discuss the most generic case with arbitrary
q
, we therefore focus on the
q=4
case. Then the above contribution uces to

\displaystyle 2^{2N}e^{-\frac{2JTN}{q2^{q-1}}+\text{i}N\sigma^{2}T^{2}/4}\ .

(446)

Recall that in the end we need to perform the integral (
410
), as we argued in last section we have to deform the contour to a steepest contour such that the (
446
) vanishes at infinity. It means that this saddle point behaves like

\displaystyle 2^{2N}e^{-\frac{2JTN}{q2^{q-1}}-N|\text{Im}(\sigma^{2})T^{2}/4}\ .

(447)

so it is sub-dominant comparing with the trivial saddle. Therefore in the regime of time,
\Phi
is still self-averaging.

Long time

At very long time we rewrite the
\log
term as

\displaystyle\log\left(e^{\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{+}}+e^{-\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{+}}+e^{\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{-}}+e^{\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{-}}\right)\,.

(448)

When
T
is sufficiently large, only one term in the above expression is dominant in the
\log
function, there are thus two different cases to be discussed; either the term that is independent of
\sigma
or the term that depend on
\sigma
. When the
\sigma
independent term dominates, the saddle point contribution is independent on
\sigma
, which is not related to either the wormhole or half-wormhole contribution that we are interested in.
Therefore it is tentative to consider the case where the
\sigma
dependent term dominates

\displaystyle\log\left(e^{\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{+}}+e^{-\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{+}}+e^{\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{-}}+e^{\text{i}Tf^{\prime}_{-}}\right)\approx{\frac{\text{i}aT}{2}\sqrt{e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}(\sigma_{14}+\sigma_{23})^{2}-(\sigma_{13}-\sigma_{24})^{2}+e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}4\sigma^{2}}},

where
a=\pm 1
. The saddle point equations are

\displaystyle 2g_{13}+\frac{\text{i}ag_{13}^{q-1}}{\sqrt{g_{13}^{2q-2}-e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}g_{14}^{2q-2}+e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}=0,

(449)

\displaystyle 2g_{14}-\frac{\text{i}ae^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}g_{14}^{q-1}}{\sqrt{g_{13}^{2q-2}-e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}g_{14}^{2q-2}+e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}\tilde{\sigma}^{2}}}=0,

(450)

\displaystyle g_{14}=g_{23}\,,\quad g_{24}=-g_{13}\,,\quad\sigma_{14}=\sigma_{23}\,,\quad\sigma_{24}=-\sigma_{13}\ .

(451)

where
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}=\sigma^{2}/J^{2}
and the equation of motion (
439
) has been used. Solutions of this set of equations are complicated in general, here we only provide the solutions for
q=4
. First let us consider the non-trivial solutions
g_{ab}\neq 0
. There is a set of 8 solutions

\displaystyle g_{13}=\pm e^{\frac{\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}}\,,\quad g_{14}=\pm e^{\frac{7\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}}\,,\quad g_{13}=\pm e^{\frac{\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}}\,,\quad g_{14}=\mp e^{\frac{7\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}},

(452)

\displaystyle g_{13}=\pm e^{\frac{5\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}}\,,\quad g_{14}=\pm e^{\frac{3\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}}\,,\quad g_{13}=\pm e^{\frac{5\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}}\,,\quad g_{14}=\mp e^{\frac{3\text{i}\pi}{8}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\sigma}}{a}}\,,

(453)

where all the unlisted variables are given by (
451
).
The first four solutions lead to the same on-shell action

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}e^{\text{i}aTN\sqrt{e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}\sigma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{4JTN}{q2^{q}}}\,,

(454)

while the last four saddles lead to another on-shell action

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}e^{\text{i}aTN\sqrt{e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}\sigma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{4JTN}{q2^{q}}}\ .

(455)

Comparing with the contribution from the trivial saddles

\displaystyle\langle\Phi^{2}\rangle=\left(2\cos\left(Te^{-\frac{\text{i}\pi}{q}}\sigma\right)\right)^{N}e^{-\frac{2JTN}{q}}\sim e^{\pm\text{i}TN\sqrt{e^{-\frac{2\text{i}\pi}{q}}\sigma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{4JTN}{2^{q}q}}\,,

(456)

we find that all of them are comparable with the trivial saddle. We have seen this phenomenon in the 0-dimensional SYK model. We expect these non-trivial saddles to not contribute to the path integral, which could be checked in the Lefschetz thimble analysis.

In the end let us consider the special non-trivial solution with
g_{23}=0=g_{14}
. Focusing on the case of
a=1
, the saddle point which has the proper fall-off behavior at infinite of
\sigma
is

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{3}{2}NT\sigma^{\frac{4}{3}}\right),\quad\sigma\rightarrow\infty.

(457)

Then we plot the region where this non-trivial saddle dominates over the trivial saddle in Fig.
13
.

Figure 13
:
The shaded region is where non-trivial saddle dominates. We have set
J=1/2
.

It turns out that wormhole saddle
\sigma_{wh}=\pm\frac{1}{2}\text{i}e^{\frac{\text{i}\pi}{4}}
is not in this region. For example at
J=1/2
,
\text{Re}(\langle\Phi^{2}(\sigma_{wh})\rangle_{\text{non-trivial}}-\langle\Phi^{2}(\sigma_{wh})\rangle_{\text{trivial}})=-0.23
.
It suggests that like the 0-dimensional model, the wormhole saddle of
\langle|z|^{2}\rangle
are within the self-averaging region of the
\sigma
plane.

8.5
Half-wormholes

With all the results in the previous sections, we expect that the integral (
410
) can be approximated by

\displaystyle|z|^{2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}-0}\frac{\text{d}\sigma}{2\pi}\Psi(\sigma)\Phi(\sigma)+\left(\text{ half-wormholes at }\sigma=0\right),

(458)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}-0}\frac{\text{d}\sigma}{2\pi}\Psi(\sigma)\langle\Phi(\sigma)\rangle+\left(\text{ half-wormholes at }\sigma=0\right),

(459)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle|z|^{2}\rangle+\Phi(0)\,,

(460)

at late time. First we notice that in the late time

\displaystyle\langle\Phi(0)\rangle=2^{N}e^{-\frac{4JTN}{q2^{q}}}\rightarrow 0

(461)

therefore at least (
460
) is consistent. To confirm (
460
) we need to compute
\langle\Phi(0)^{2}\rangle
,
\langle\Phi(0)|z|\rangle
and the error:

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\langle(|z|^{2}-\langle|z|^{2}\rangle-\Phi(0))^{2}\rangle=\langle|z|^{4}\rangle-\langle|z|^{2}\rangle^{2}+\langle\Phi(0)^{2}\rangle-2\langle|z|^{2}\Phi(0)\rangle.

(462)

In the late time, each term in (
462
) is given by the non-trivial saddle points. It is clear that
\langle\Phi(0)^{2}\rangle
,
\langle\Phi(0)|z|\rangle
can be obtain from
\langle|z|^{4}\rangle
by setting
g_{12}=0
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\begin{cases}(3-1+2-4)\langle z^{2}\rangle^{2}_{WH}=0&q=4k+2,\\
(2-1+1-2)\langle z^{2}\rangle^{2}_{WH}=0&q=4k,\end{cases}

(463)

therefore (
460
) indeed is good in the large
T
limit.

9
Modified Brownian SYK model

In this section, we study the wormhole and half-wormholes contributions in some modified (Brownian) SYK model.

9.1
Brownian SYK with non-vanishing mean value

Let us first consider to turn on the mean value of the random couplings:

\displaystyle\langle J_{A}\rangle=J_{A}^{(0)}=\mu,\quad\langle J_{A}(t)J_{B}(t^{\prime})\rangle=\delta(t-t^{\prime})(\delta_{AB}\tau^{2}+\mu^{2})\,,

(464)

and in this section we use
the convention
\{\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\}=2h\delta_{i,j}
.

Taking the disorder averaging of the coupling we obtain the averaged theory

\displaystyle\langle z(T)\rangle_{J}

\displaystyle=\int d^{N}\psi\,e^{-S_{a}}\,,

(465)

\displaystyle S_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\sum_{i}^{N}\psi_{i}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}-\text{i}^{q/2}\int dt\sum_{A}J_{A}^{(0)}\psi_{A}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\int dt(\sum_{A}\psi_{A}^{2})

(466)

We can convert the effective Hamiltonian of the averaged theory as a spin system

\displaystyle\langle z\rangle_{J}=\text{Tr}(e^{-T{\cal H}})\,,\quad{\cal H}=-\text{i}^{q/2}\sum_{A}J_{A}^{(0)}\psi_{A}-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\sum_{A}h^{q}=-\text{i}^{q/2}\sum_{A}J_{A}^{(0)}\psi_{A}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}{N\choose q}h^{q}\ .

(467)

When
\mu=0
, the averaged partition function is given by

\displaystyle\langle z\rangle_{J}=e^{T\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}{N\choose q}h^{q}}\equiv 2^{N}e^{TE_{0}},\quad E_{0}=\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}{N\choose q}h^{q}\sim\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}N^{q}h^{q}\,.

(468)

When
\mu\neq 0
, we have to evaluate the trace

\displaystyle\langle z\rangle_{J}=e^{TE_{0}}\text{Tr}(e^{T\text{i}^{q/2}\mu\sum_{A}\psi_{A}})=e^{TE_{0}}\int\text{d}^{N}\psi_{i}\exp(T\text{i}^{q/2}\sum_{i}\psi_{i})\ .

(469)

However there is no simple expression for
\langle z\rangle
. We first consider the simplest case with
q=1

\displaystyle I_{f}=\int\text{d}^{N}\psi_{i}\exp(a\sum_{i}\psi_{i})\ .

(470)

The idea is to transfer the Majorana fermions to Dirac fermions which have a well-defined rules of integrals. Assuming the total number of fermions is even
N=2K
then we introduce
K
Dirac fermions as

\displaystyle c_{i}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{h}}(\psi_{2i-1}-\text{i}\psi_{2i}),\quad c_{i}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{h}}(\psi_{2i-1}+\text{i}\psi_{2i}),\quad i=1,\dots,K\,,

(471)

\displaystyle\psi_{2i-1}=\sqrt{h}(c_{i}+c_{i}^{\dagger}),\quad\psi_{2i}=\text{i}\sqrt{h}(c_{i}-c_{i}^{\dagger})\,,

(472)

which obey

\displaystyle\{c_{i},c_{j}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{c_{i}^{\dagger},c_{j}^{\dagger}\}=0,\quad\{c_{i},c_{j}^{\dagger}\}=\delta_{ij}\,

(473)

The integration measure changes as

\displaystyle{\cal D}\psi_{2i}{\cal D}\psi_{2i-1}=2h{\cal D}c_{i}{\cal D}c_{i}^{\dagger}\,.

(475)

Thus the integral can be evaluated as

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2h)^{K}\int\prod_{i}{\cal D}c_{i}{\cal D}c_{i}^{\dagger}\exp\left(a\sum_{i}^{K}\sqrt{h}[(1+\text{i})c_{i}+(1-\text{i})c_{i}^{\dagger}]\right)

(476)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2h)^{K}(2\cosh(\sqrt{2}ah))^{K}=(2h)^{N}[\cosh(\sqrt{2}ah)]^{N/2}\,.

(477)

Now we let us consider the case of
q=2

\displaystyle I_{2}(a)=\int\text{d}^{N}\psi_{i}\exp(\frac{a}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}\psi_{i}A_{ij}\psi_{j}),\quad\text{with }\quad\left(A_{ij}=-A_{ij}=a,\quad i<j\right),

(478)

which looks like a Gaussian but we need to replace
\psi_{i}
with
c_{i}
:

\displaystyle I_{2}=(\sqrt{2h})^{N}\int\prod_{i}{\cal D}c_{i}{\cal D}c_{i}^{\dagger}\,e^{{\cal H}}

(479)

\displaystyle\quad{\cal H}=\left(\text{i}ah(\sum_{i}^{K}[c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{i}-c_{i}c_{i}^{\dagger}]+2\sum_{i<j}[c_{i}c_{j}-c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{j}^{\dagger}])+2ah\sum_{i<j}[c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{j}+c_{i}c_{j}^{\dagger}]\right)

(480)

To get an idea how to compute this integral let us consider a simple case of
N=4
:

\displaystyle\psi_{1}=\sqrt{h}(c_{1}+c_{1}^{\dagger}),\psi_{2}=\text{i}\sqrt{h}(c_{1}-c_{1}^{\dagger}),\psi_{3}=\sqrt{h}(c_{2}+c_{2}^{\dagger}),\psi_{4}=\text{i}\sqrt{h}(c_{2}-c_{2}^{\dagger}),

(481)

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=\text{i}h(c_{1}^{\dagger}c_{1}-c_{1}c_{1}^{\dagger}+c_{2}^{\dagger}c_{2}-c_{2}c_{2}^{\dagger}+2c_{1}c_{2}-2c_{1}^{\dagger}c_{2}^{\dagger})+2h(c_{1}^{\dagger}c_{2}+c_{1}c_{2}^{\dagger}).

(482)

We have four different states
|\Psi_{i}\rangle
:

\displaystyle|00\rangle,\quad c_{1}^{\dagger}|00\rangle,\quad c_{2}^{\dagger}|00\rangle,\quad c_{1}^{\dagger}c_{2}^{\dagger}|00\rangle.

(483)

So the operator
\sum_{i<j}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}
can be written as a
4\times 4
matrix :

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}-2\text{i}h&0&0&-2\text{i}h\\
0&0&-2h&0\\
0&2h&0&0\\
-2\text{i}h&0&0&2\text{i}h\\
\end{array}\right)

with 4 eigenvalues
\{\pm\text{i}2h,\pm\text{i}2\sqrt{2}h\}
so path integral over
c_{i}
and
c_{i}^{\dagger}
can be computed as

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\langle\Psi_{i}|e^{a\sum_{i}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}}|\Psi_{i}\rangle=2\left(\cos(2ah)+\cos(2\sqrt{2}ah)\right)\,.

(489)

For example of
N=6
, the corresponding matrix is

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccc}-3\text{i}h&0&0&0&-2\text{i}h&-2\text{i}h&-2\text{i}h&0\\
0&-\text{i}h&2h&2h&0&0&0&-2\text{i}h\\
0&-2h&-\text{i}h&2h&0&0&0&2\text{i}h\\
0&-2h&-2h&-\text{i}h&0&0&0&-2\text{i}h\\
-2\text{i}h&0&0&0&\text{i}h&2h&-2h&0\\
-2\text{i}h&0&0&0&-2h&\text{i}h&2h&0\\
-2\text{i}h&0&0&0&2h&-2h&\text{i}h&0\\
0&-2\text{i}h&2\text{i}h&-2\text{i}h&0&0&0&3\text{i}h\\
\end{array}\right)

which can be divided into two blocks. We get the eigenvalues by directly diagonalizing the matrix:

\displaystyle\pm 5\text{i}h,\quad\pm(2\sqrt{3}+1)\text{i}h,\quad\pm 3\text{i}h,\quad\pm(2\sqrt{3}-1)\text{i}h.

(499)

Similarly for general
N
, we can write effective Hamiltonian defined in (
479
) as

\displaystyle{\cal H}=\sum_{i\leq j=1}^{k}\left(\alpha_{ij}c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{j}+\beta_{ij}c_{i}c_{j}^{\dagger}+\gamma_{ij}c_{i}^{\dagger}c_{j}^{\dagger}+\theta_{ij}c_{i}c_{j}\right),

(500)

with

\displaystyle\alpha_{ii}=\text{i}h,\quad\beta_{ii}=-\text{i}h,\quad\alpha_{ij}=2h,\quad\beta_{ij}=2h,

(501)

\displaystyle\gamma_{ij}=-2\text{i}h,\quad\theta_{ij}=2\text{i}h,\quad\gamma_{ii}=0,\quad\theta_{ii}=0.

(502)

This Hamiltonian is quadratic and famously can be diagonalized by the Bogoliubov and Valatin’s method
Bogolyubov:1947zz
;
Valatin:1958ja
. Explicitly we can do the transformation by taking an operator basis for the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H=c^{\dagger}Mc

(503)

where we have

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}=\left(c_{1}^{\dagger},c_{2}^{\dagger},\dots,c_{1},c_{2},\dots\right).

(504)

In the simple case with
N=4
the matrix can be expressed as

\displaystyle M=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\text{i}h&h&0&-\text{i}h\\
-h&\text{i}h&\text{i}h&0\\
0&\text{i}h&-\text{i}h&h\\
-\text{i}h&0&-h&-\text{i}h\\
\end{array}\right),

we can directly take the diagonalization and get the eigenvalues

\displaystyle\text{i}(1+\sqrt{2})h,\quad-\text{i}(1+\sqrt{2})h,\quad-\text{i}(1-\sqrt{2})h,\quad-\text{i}(-1+\sqrt{2})h.

(510)

For simplicity we take the notation as

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=\text{i}(\sqrt{2}+1)h,\quad\lambda_{2}=\text{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)h,

(511)

then the resulting effective Hamiltonian becomes

\displaystyle H=\lambda_{1}\left(d_{1}^{\dagger}d_{1}-d_{1}d_{1}^{\dagger}\right)+\lambda_{2}\left(d_{2}^{\dagger}d_{2}-d_{2}d_{2}^{\dagger}\right).

(512)

To evaluate the trace we still take the states as (
483
) therefore we have

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(e^{H})=e^{-a(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})}+e^{a(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})}+e^{a(-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})}+e^{a(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})},

(513)

so we can recover the result (
489
). For general
N
the operator (
500
) can be expressed as a block matrix

\displaystyle M=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A+\text{i}hI_{N}&-\text{i}A\\
\text{i}A&A-\text{i}hI_{N}\end{array}\right),

with

\displaystyle A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&h&h&\cdots\\
-h&0&h&\cdots\\
-h&-h&0&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots\end{array}\right)

The characteristic equation is

\displaystyle\det\left(A+(\text{i}h-\lambda))(A-(\text{i}h+\lambda)-H^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\det\left((h^{2}+\lambda^{2})I_{N}-2\lambda A\right)

(521)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda+h)^{N}+(\lambda-h)^{N}=0\,.

(522)

So the eigenvalues are

\displaystyle\lambda_{m}=\text{i}h\tan(\frac{m\pi}{2N}),\quad m=1,3,\dots,N-1\,.

(523)

then the Hamiltonian becomes

\displaystyle H=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}(d_{i}^{\dagger}d_{i}-d_{i}d_{i}^{\dagger}).

(524)

and the trace will have the form

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}(e^{H})=\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}e^{a\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}\lambda_{i}}=\sum_{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{k}e^{a\sigma_{i}\lambda_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\sigma}e^{a\sigma_{i}\lambda_{i}}=2^{k}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\cosh\left(a\lambda_{i}\right)\,,

(525)

Now let us consider the function

\displaystyle X_{n}=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\dots i_{n}\leq N}\psi_{i_{1}}\dots\psi_{i_{N}}.

(526)

We would like to argue that in the large
N
limit, we have the approximation

\displaystyle n!X_{2n}\approx(X_{2})^{n},

(527)

as we find for the 0-dimensional theory. Note that unlike the situation of the 0-dimensional theory,
\{X_{n}\}
do not form a basis for
X_{2}^{n}
. For example, let us take
N=6
, there is indeed the identity

\displaystyle X_{2}^{2}=-15+2!X_{4}

(528)

but we find that

\displaystyle X_{2}^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3!X_{6}+15X_{2}+12(\psi_{1}\psi_{2}+\psi_{1}\psi_{6}+\psi_{3}\psi_{4}+\psi_{4}\psi_{5}+\psi_{5}\psi_{6})

(529)

\displaystyle\qquad-4(\psi_{1}\psi_{4}+\psi_{2}\psi_{4}+\psi_{3}\psi_{6}).

Let us focus on the second last term in
X_{2}^{n}

\displaystyle X_{2}^{n}\approx\dots c_{1}X_{2n-4}+n!X_{2n},\quad c_{1}=(n-2)!{n\choose 2}{N\choose 2}\,,

(530)

where
c_{1}
is computed as follows. We need to pick 2
X_{2}
out of
n
and contract them, and the
(n-2)

X_{2}
’s remain not contracted and gives
(n-1)!

X_{2n-4}
. Notice that the subleading term is
X_{2n-4}
instead of
X_{2n-2}
, since if we contract one fermion in
X_{2}
to get

\displaystyle\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\psi_{1}\psi_{3}\mapsto\psi_{3}\psi_{2}\,,

(531)

there is going to be another contraction that gives

\displaystyle\psi_{1}\psi_{3}\psi_{1}\psi_{2}\mapsto\psi_{2}\psi_{3}\ .

(532)

The two outcomes simply cancel with each other. The main conclusion of this computation is, given that
X_{2n}\sim N^{2n}
, the subleading terms can be safely neglected and approximate
X_{2n}
by
X_{2}^{n}
. So in the large
N
limit, we can use the
G,\Sigma
trick to compute the fermionic integral

\displaystyle I_{q}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{N}\psi_{i}\exp(a\sum_{A}\psi_{A}),\quad A=\{1\leq a_{1}<\dots<a_{q}\leq N\},

(533)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{N}\psi_{i}\,e^{a\frac{G^{\frac{q}{2}}}{\frac{q}{2}!}}e^{\text{i}\sigma(G-\sum_{i<j}\psi_{i}\psi_{j})}\text{d}G\text{d}\sigma

(534)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}G\text{d}\sigma I_{2}(-\text{i}\sigma)e^{a\frac{G^{\frac{q}{2}}}{\frac{q}{2}!}}e^{\text{i}\sigma G}=I_{2}(\text{i}\partial_{G})e^{a\frac{G^{\frac{q}{2}}}{\frac{q}{2}!}}|_{G=0}\,.

(535)

where the function
I_{2}
is defined in (
478
). We can evaluate this expression and we expect the half-wormhole contributions to be similar as the
0
-SYK model

\displaystyle z\approx\langle z\rangle+\Theta\,,\qquad\Theta=\int d^{N}\psi\,e^{-\int_{0}^{T}dt\,\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}^{N}\psi_{i}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}+\text{i}^{q/2}\int_{0}^{T}dt\sum_{A}(J_{A}-\mu)\psi_{A}}\ .

(536)

The detailed analysis is similar to the Brownian SYK model as we have shown above, but it is not particularly illuminating, so we omit them here.

In the next section, we instead consider a modified SYK-like model where half-wormhole saddle can be verified explicitly.

9.2
Random coupling from product of Grassmann variables
J_{A}^{(0)}=J\prod_{i}\theta_{A_{i}}

A modified SYK-like model dubbed as partially disorder-averaged SYK model is proposed in
Goto:2021wfs
. In this model, the random coupling
\widetilde{J}_{A}
consists of two pieces

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}_{A}=J_{A}+J_{A}^{(0)}

(537)

where
J_{A}
is the standard random coupling of the SYK model while
J_{A}^{(0)}
is specially chosen as

\displaystyle J_{i_{i}\dots i_{q}}^{(0)}=\text{i}^{q/2}q!\mu\,\theta_{i_{1}}\dots\theta_{i_{q}},\quad\text{with}\quad\{\theta_{i},\theta_{j}\}={2}\delta_{ij}

(538)

so we can think of it as coupling the fermions
\psi_{i}
in the original model with some background Majorana fermions
\theta_{i}
(or non-dynamical fermions living in another universe
Goto:2021wfs
). Note that
J_{A}^{(0)}
is not a c-number which is different from our models studied in the previous section.

9.2.1
0d model

Let us first consider the
0
-dimensional model to see the difference explicitly. In this case the integral (
154
) can be written as

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\exp(\text{i}^{q/2}\sum\widetilde{J}_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}}\psi_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}})\,

(539)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\exp\left(\text{i}^{q/2}\sum_{A}J_{A}\psi_{A}+\mu(\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i})^{q}\right).

The averaged quantity
\langle z\rangle

\displaystyle\langle z\rangle=\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\exp\left(\mu(\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i})^{q}\right)\,,

(540)

can be computed in two ways. One can integrate out the fermions
\psi_{i}
directly. The result is

\displaystyle\langle z\rangle=\frac{\mu^{N/q}N!}{(N/q)!}\int\text{d}^{N}\psi(\theta_{1}\psi_{1})\dots(\theta_{N}\psi_{N})=\left[\prod_{i}\theta_{i}\right]\frac{\mu^{N/q}N!}{(N/q)!}\equiv\left[\prod_{i}\theta_{i}\right]\mathfrak{m}_{p}\,.

(541)

Note that
z
is not a c-number and depends on the background fermions living in other universe. Here we will not think of this as a problem but a feature since the model is not exactly the original SYK model. Alternatively we can compute this average quantity by the
G,\Sigma
trick:

\displaystyle G_{\sigma}=\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}\,,

\displaystyle\langle z\rangle=\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\int\text{d}G_{\sigma}\frac{\text{d}\Sigma_{\sigma}}{2\pi}e^{\text{i}[\Sigma_{\sigma}(G_{\sigma}-\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i})]}e^{\mu G_{\sigma}^{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\left[\prod_{i}\theta_{i}\right]\int\text{d}G_{\sigma}\frac{\text{d}\Sigma_{\sigma}}{2\pi}\Sigma_{\sigma}^{N}e^{\text{i}\Sigma_{\sigma}G_{\sigma}+\mu G_{\sigma}^{q}}

(542)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left[\prod_{i}\theta_{i}\right](\partial_{G_{\sigma}})^{N}e^{\mu G_{\sigma}^{q}}|_{G_{\sigma}=0}=\left[\prod_{i}\theta_{i}\right]\frac{\mu^{N/q}N!}{(N/q)!}\,.

(543)

One can also use the effective action (
542
) to derive the large
N
result of (
540
) as shown in
Goto:2021wfs
. We will not repeat that analysis here. Instead, we would like to consider the half-wormhole saddle of
z

\displaystyle z\approx\langle z\rangle+\Theta

(544)

as we did in last section. The subtlety is that as we stressed
z
is not a c-number so the approximation (
544
) is in the sense

\displaystyle\langle[z-(\langle z\rangle+\Theta)]^{2}\rangle\approx 0\,,

(545)

which is a c-number due to (
538
) is small. Let us proceed by computing the averaged quantity
\langle z^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\langle z^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi\,\exp\left({\frac{\tau^{2}}{q!}}(\sum_{i}\psi_{i}^{L}\psi_{i}^{R})^{q}+\mu(\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{L})^{q}+\mu(\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{R})^{q}\right)

(546)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi\sum_{k}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{q!}\right)^{k}\sum_{{i_{1}<\dots<i_{kq}}}(\psi^{LR}_{i_{1}}\dots\psi^{LR}_{i_{qk}})\frac{(qk)!}{k!}\left(\frac{(N-kq)!}{(N/q-k)!}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\mu^{2p-2k}\sum_{j_{1}<\dots<j_{N-qk}\neq\{i\}}(\theta_{j_{1}}\psi_{j_{1}}^{L})\dots(\theta_{j_{N-qk}}\psi^{L}_{j_{N-kq}})(\theta_{j_{1}}\psi_{j_{1}}^{R})\dots(\theta_{j_{N-k}}\psi^{R}_{j_{N-kq}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi\sum_{k}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{q!}\right)^{k}\mu^{2p-2k}\frac{(qk)!}{k!}\left(\frac{(N-kq)!}{(N/q-k)!}\right)^{2}{N\choose kq}\psi^{LR}_{1}\dots\psi_{N}^{LR}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{q!}\right)^{k}\mu^{2p-2k}\frac{(qk)!}{k!}\left(\frac{(N-kq)!}{(N/q-k)!}\right)^{2}{N\choose kq}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}^{p}\tau^{2k}\mu^{2(p-k)}c_{k}\mathfrak{m}_{p-k}^{2}\equiv\sum_{k}\mathfrak{z}_{2}^{(k)}\,,

(547)

where
c_{k}
of defined in (
231
) and
\mathfrak{m}_{p}
is defined in (
541
). The result (
546
) is in the same form of (
230
). So the analysis of the half-wormhole saddle will be similar; we insert the a suitable identity to (
539
)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\exp(\text{i}^{q/2}\sum\widetilde{J}_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}}\psi_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}})\int\text{d}G_{\sigma}\delta(G_{\sigma}-\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i})\exp(\frac{\mu}{q!}(G_{\sigma}^{q}-(\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i})^{q}))

(548)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\frac{\text{d}\Sigma_{\sigma}\text{d}G_{\sigma}}{2\pi\text{i}}\exp(\text{i}^{q/2}\sum_{A}J_{A}\psi_{A}+\Sigma_{\sigma}\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i})\exp(-\Sigma_{\sigma}G_{\sigma}+\frac{\mu}{q!}G_{\sigma}^{q})\,.

Following the arguments below (
4.1
) one can obtain the half-wormhole saddle
16
16

16

Here the factor
\prod_{i}\theta_{i}
should be present.

\displaystyle\Theta=\left[\prod_{i}\theta_{i}\right]\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\exp(\text{i}^{q/2}\sum_{A}J_{A}\psi_{A})\,.

(549)

Then it is easy to find that the half-wormhole saddle satisfies

\displaystyle\langle\Theta\rangle=0,\quad\langle\Theta^{2}\rangle=\langle\Theta z\rangle=\mathfrak{z}_{2}^{(p)}\,,

(550)

so the approximation (
544
) will be sufficient if
\mathfrak{z}_{2}^{(p)}
is the dominant term in (
547
) as we have shown in last section. When
\mathfrak{z}_{2}^{(p)}
is not the dominant term we have to consider the contribution of fluctuation of
\Sigma_{\sigma}
. To finish our analysis of the half-wormhole saddle for
z
, let us redo the computation of
\langle z^{2}\rangle
with the
G,\Sigma
trick. We need introduce three
G
variables

\displaystyle G_{LR}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}\psi_{i}^{L}\psi_{i}^{R},\quad G_{L}=\frac{1}{{N}}\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{L},\quad G_{R}=\frac{1}{{N}}\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{R}

(551)

then
\langle z^{2}\rangle
can be written as

\displaystyle\langle z^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi\prod_{a}\text{d}G_{a}\exp\left(\frac{N}{q}(tG_{LR}^{q}+uG_{L}^{q}+uG_{R}^{q})\right)\int\prod_{a}\frac{d\Sigma_{a}}{2\pi\text{i}/N}\exp(-\Sigma_{LR}(NG_{LR}-\sum_{i}\psi_{i}^{L}\psi_{i}^{R}))

(552)

\displaystyle\exp(-\Sigma_{L}(NG_{L}-\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{L}))\exp(-\Sigma_{R}(NG_{R}-\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{R}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int[\prod_{a}\text{d}G_{a}\frac{d\Sigma_{a}}{2\pi\text{i}/N}]\exp\left(N(\frac{t}{q}G_{LR}^{q}+\frac{u}{q}G_{L}^{q}+\frac{u}{q}G_{R}^{q}-\sum_{a}\Sigma_{a}G_{a})\right)(\Sigma_{LR}+\Sigma_{L}\Sigma_{R})^{N}\,,

where in order to have a well-defined large
N
scaling we have introduced

\displaystyle t=\frac{\tau^{2}}{(q-1)!}N^{q-1},\quad u=q\mu N^{q-1}.

(553)

The saddle point equations are

\displaystyle tG_{LR}^{q-1}=\Sigma_{LR},\quad uG_{L}^{q-1}=\Sigma_{L},\quad uG_{R}^{q-1}=\Sigma_{R}\,,

(554)

\displaystyle G_{LR}=\frac{1}{\Sigma_{LR}+\Sigma_{L}\Sigma_{R}},\quad G_{L}=-\frac{\Sigma_{R}}{\Sigma_{LR}+\Sigma_{L}\Sigma_{R}},\quad G_{R}=-\frac{\Sigma_{L}}{\Sigma_{LR}+\Sigma_{L}\Sigma_{R}}\,.

(555)

The obvious solutions are the “wormhole” saddles with

\displaystyle G_{L}=G_{R}=\Sigma_{L}=\Sigma_{R}=0,

(556)

which corresponds to
\mathfrak{z}_{2}^{p}
. There are also other saddles corresponding to other
\mathfrak{z}_{2}^{k}
. For the simplest case
q=2
, these solutions can be written explicitly. The “wormhole” saddles are

\displaystyle G_{L}=G_{R}=\Sigma_{L}=\Sigma_{R}=0,G_{LR}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}},\quad\Sigma_{LR}=\pm\sqrt{t},

(557)

\displaystyle\langle z^{2}\rangle_{\text{WH}}=e^{-\frac{N}{2}}t^{N/2}\,,

(558)

which do not depend on
\mu
and the other four solutions are

\displaystyle\Sigma_{L}=\Sigma_{R}=uG_{L}=uG_{R}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{u^{2}-t}{u}},\quad\Sigma_{LR}=\frac{t}{u},\quad G_{LR}=\frac{1}{u}

(559)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{L}=uG_{L}=-\Sigma_{R}=-uG_{R}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{u^{2}-t}{u}},\quad\Sigma_{LR}=-\frac{{t}}{u},\quad G_{LR}=-\frac{1}{u}\,,

(560)

\displaystyle\langle z^{2}\rangle_{\text{new}}=e^{-\frac{N}{2}(2-\frac{t}{u^{2}})}u^{N}

(561)

Apparently when
u\rightarrow\infty
,
\Sigma_{LR},G_{LR}\rightarrow 0,
then we expect that in this limit the dominant saddle will correspond to
\mathfrak{z}_{2}^{0}
since in this limit saddle point value does not depend on
t
. Comparing these two saddle values we find

\displaystyle\frac{\langle z^{2}\rangle_{\text{WH}}}{\langle z^{2}\rangle_{\text{new}}}=\exp\left(\frac{N}{2}(1-x+\log x)\right)\leq 1,\quad x=\frac{t}{u^{2}}\,.

(562)

Note that when
x=1
such that
\langle z^{2}\rangle_{\text{WH}}=\langle z^{2}\rangle_{\text{new}}
the new saddle just reduces to the wormhole saddle. Therefore it implies that the new saddle always dominates.

This new saddle is named as “unlinked half-wormhole” in
Goto:2021wfs
to distinguish it from the half-wormhole saddle which was found in
Saad:2021rcu
. One interpretation of this new saddle is that it is the analogue of the disconnected saddle in this model; indeed, we do not find other disconnected saddle with
G_{LR}=0
,
\Sigma_{LR}=0
and
G_{L/R},\Sigma_{L/R}\neq 0
, in addition, this saddle is present only when
u\neq 0
, and this saddle is more and more important as
u
increases.

The analysis of the half-wormhole saddle for
z^{2}
will be similar to one we did in last section so we will not repeat here.

9.2.2
1d model

Now we come back to the 0+1d model that is a variant of the Brownian SYK model. Let us begin by deriving the wormhole saddle of
\langle z^{2}\rangle
17
17

17

Here we have assumed the large
N
limit, the exact treatment can be found in
Saad:2021rcu

\displaystyle z_{L}z_{R}=\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi\exp\left\{-\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(\psi_{i}^{L}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}^{L}+\psi_{i}^{R}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}^{R})+\text{i}^{q/2}\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\sum_{A}\tilde{J}_{A}(\psi_{A}^{L}+\psi_{A}^{R})\right\}

\displaystyle\langle z_{L}z_{R}\rangle=\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi\exp\left\{-\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(\psi_{i}^{L}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}^{L}+\psi_{i}^{R}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}^{R})+\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\left({\frac{\tau^{2}}{q!}}(\sum_{i}\psi_{i}^{L}\psi_{i}^{R})^{q}+\mu(\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{L})^{q}+\mu(\sum_{i}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{R})^{q}\right)+\tau^{2}E_{0}T\right\}\,.

(563)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi[\prod_{a}\text{d}G_{a}\frac{d\Sigma_{a}}{2\pi\text{i}}]\exp\left\{-\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(\psi_{i}^{L}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}^{L}+\psi_{i}^{R}\partial_{t}\psi_{i}^{R})+\tau^{2}TE_{0}+\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.\int_{0}^{T}\text{d}t\left(\frac{\tau^{2}}{q!}G_{LR}^{q}+\mu G_{L}^{q}+\mu G_{R}^{q}-\sum_{a}\Sigma_{a}G_{a}+\sum_{i}[\Sigma_{LR}\psi_{i}^{L}\psi_{i}^{R}+\Sigma_{L}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{L}+\Sigma_{R}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{R}]\right)\right\}\,,

where
E_{0}={N\choose q}
is the constant term coming from
\psi_{A}^{L(R)}\psi_{A}^{L(R)}=(-1)^{\frac{q}{2}}
. As explained in
Saad:2021rcu
, we can focus on the time-independent saddles then the fermions can be simply integrated out. The result is
18
18

18

This is result is different from the one derived in
Goto:2021wfs
. It seems that they used a wrong formula for the fermion integral.

\displaystyle\langle z_{L}z_{R}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{T\tau^{2}E_{0}}\int[\prod_{a}\text{d}G_{a}\frac{d\Sigma_{a}}{2\pi\text{i}}]e^{TN(\frac{t}{q}G_{LR}^{q}+\frac{u}{q}G_{L}^{q}+\frac{u}{q}G_{R}^{q}-\sum_{a}\Sigma_{a}G_{a})}[\cosh(T\sqrt{\Sigma_{L}^{2}+\Sigma_{R}^{2}-\Sigma_{LR}^{2}})]^{N}.

(565)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int[\prod_{a}\text{d}G_{a}\frac{d\Sigma_{a}}{2\pi\text{i}}]e^{\tau^{2}TE_{0}}e^{S_{eff}}

For general
T
, the saddle equation is very hard to solve due to the complicity of
\cosh
function. However the equations simplify in the large
T
limit because of the following approximations

\displaystyle\log(\cosh(T\sqrt{\Sigma_{L}^{2}+\Sigma_{R}^{2}-\Sigma_{LR}^{2}}))\approx\pm\text{i}T\sqrt{\Sigma_{LR}^{2}-\Sigma_{L}^{2}-\Sigma_{R}^{2}}\,.

(566)

Then in this limit the effective action becomes

\displaystyle S_{eff}=TN(\frac{t}{q}G_{LR}^{q}+\frac{u}{q}G_{L}^{q}+\frac{u}{q}G_{R}^{q}-\sum_{a}\Sigma_{a}G_{a})\pm\text{i}NT\sqrt{\Sigma_{LR}^{2}-\Sigma_{L}^{2}-\Sigma_{R}^{2}}\,,

(567)

and corresponding saddle point equations are

\displaystyle tG_{LR}^{q-1}=\Sigma_{LR},\quad uG_{L}^{q-1}=\Sigma_{L},\quad uG_{R}^{q-1}=\Sigma_{R},

(568)

\displaystyle G_{LR}=\pm\frac{\text{i}\Sigma_{LR}}{\sqrt{\Sigma_{LR}^{2}-\Sigma_{L}^{2}-\Sigma_{R}^{2}}}\,,

(569)

\displaystyle G_{L}=\mp\frac{\text{i}\Sigma_{L}}{\sqrt{\Sigma_{LR}^{2}-\Sigma_{L}^{2}-\Sigma_{R}^{2}}},\quad G_{R}=\mp\frac{\text{i}\Sigma_{R}}{\sqrt{\Sigma_{LR}^{2}-\Sigma_{L}^{2}-\Sigma_{R}^{2}}}\,.

(570)

So the wormhole saddle still presents
Saad:2018bqo

\displaystyle G_{L}=G_{R}=\Sigma_{L}=\Sigma_{R}=0,\quad G_{LR}=\pm\text{i},

(571)

\displaystyle e^{S_{eff}}\Big|_{\text{WH}}=e^{\text{i}^{q}TN\frac{t}{q}}.

(572)

The unlinked half-wormhole saddle is:

\displaystyle G_{LR}=\Sigma_{LR}=0,\quad G_{L}=\sin\alpha,\quad G_{R}=\cos\alpha,

(573)

\displaystyle e^{S_{eff}}\Big|_{\text{unlink}}=e^{TN\frac{u}{q}(\cos^{q}\alpha+\sin^{q}\alpha)}\leq e^{S_{eff}}\Big|_{\text{unlink},\alpha=0,\pi/2}=e^{TN\frac{u}{q}}\,

(574)

where the relation

\displaystyle G_{L}^{2}+G_{R}^{2}-G_{LR}^{2}=1\,,

(575)

is fulfilled and
\alpha
satisfies

\displaystyle\cos\alpha=\pm\frac{\cos^{q-1}\alpha}{\sqrt{\cos^{2q-2}\alpha+\sin^{2q-2}\alpha}}.

(576)

In the late time
(T\rightarrow\infty)
, there is indeed a wormhole saddle so it possible to include a linked half-wormhole saddle for
z
. We also assume that the half-wormhole saddle is time independent since the wormhole saddle is time independent. Then the analysis is completely same as the one for the 0-dimensional model. So the half-wormhole saddle will be given by

\displaystyle\Theta=\left[\prod_{i}\theta_{i}\right]\int\text{d}^{N}\psi\exp(T\text{i}^{q/2}\sum_{A}J_{A}\psi_{A})\,,

(577)

\displaystyle\langle\Theta^{2}\rangle\approx\langle\Theta z\rangle\approx\langle z_{L}z_{R}\rangle|_{\text{Wormhole saddle}}\,.

(578)

10
Discussion

In this paper we consider the half-wormhole proposal in some statistical models and simple SYK-like models. We showed that in all statistical models we have consider the half-wormhole conjecture (
63
) is valid almost for all the distributions except for some special cases where the mean value of the random variable vanish. In the 0-dimensional SYK model which is introduced in
Saad:2021rcu
we have shown that the half-wormhole construction depends on the distribution of the couplings. When the mean value of the coupling is very large then only the disconnected saddles dominate therefore the correlation functions automatically factorize. If the mean value is very small such that only the wormhole saddles dominate then factorization can be restored by adding half-wormhole saddles. When the disconnected saddles and wormhole saddles are comparable, we have to modified the half-wormhole saddle to restore the factorization. We also generalized
G,\Sigma
trick to compute
\langle z\rangle
. As a by-product, we can construct a a new saddle, the single half-wormhole saddle, for
z
. Moreover we argued if the random couplings satisfy a general distribution, new half-wormhole saddles can be constructed
19
19

19

Interestingly, irrelevant deformation of 0-SYK model is studied in
Das:2022uhj
where they show after deformation half-wormhole saddle survives. It is very possible that our new half-wormholes will also survive under the same irrelevant deformation.
. We also generalize the construction of half-wormhole saddles to (modified) Brownian SYK model.

Partially averaged models and spacetime branes

The meaning of higher cumulants of the random coupling can be understood from the idea of Coleman’s
Coleman:1988cy
and Giddings’s and Strominger’s
Giddings:1988wv
;
Giddings:1988cx
. Just as we showed they are related to the non-local interaction induced by the spacetime wormholes. However the first cumulant or the mean value seems to be puzzling. In
Blommaert:2021gha
;
Goto:2021wfs
, ensemble theories with non-vanishing mean value random couplings are also consider where they call such models partially averaged models. In these models, the mean values of random couplings can be understood as external sources or spacetime branes which describe the non-perturbative corrections. It is shown in
Blommaert:2021fob
by fine tuning these non-perturbative corrections the JT gravity can factorize for all orders. But it seems that the original half-wormhole
Saad:2021rcu
constructed in 0-SYK model is not related to the branes but just a result of applying
G,\Sigma
trick in the non-averaged theory. However we can understand this construction in an opposite way: the half-wormhole is constructed by adding eigenbranes
Blommaert:2019wfy
;
Goel:2020yxl
in the averaged theory. This opposite point of view can also be viewed as an explicit realization of the idea
Saad:2021uzi
about factorization.

Standard SYK model

There are already proposals
Saad:2021uzi
;
Mukhametzhanov:2021hdi
;
Mukhametzhanov:2021nea
;
Goto:2021wfs
of the half-wormhole saddle for
z^{2}
in the standard SYK model. But due to technical difficulty it has not been confirmed. It would be interesting to generalize our single half-wormhole saddle to the SYK model since
z
is much simpler than
z^{2}
. It would be also interesting to generalize the new half-wormholes we found in section
3
to the standard SYK model and understand their possible relations to saddles in JT gravity.

Non-trivial saddles and Null states

In the simple 0-dimensional SYK model and Brownian SYK model, we find some non-trivial saddles whose on-shell values are comparable with one of the trivial self-averaging saddles. It would be very interesting if such non-trivial saddle also exists in the standard SYK model. It implies that there are also some non-trivial solutions in the dual (deformed) JT gravity. In the semiclassical physics, this coexistence of bulk description can be understood as the consequence of null states. In
Blommaert:2022ucs
, the null states of (deformed) JT gravity are proposed. However these null states do not show up in the dual matrix model. It seems to be promising to identify these null states in the SYK model.
