We thank many of the members of KITS for interesting related discussions. We also want to thank Kenta Suzuki for comments on a draft of this paper.
CP is supported by the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities, by funds from the University of Chinese Academy of Science (UCAS), and funds from the Kavli Institute for Theoretical Science (KITS). JT is supported by the National Youth Fund No.12105289 and funds from the UCAS program of special research associate.

Appendix A
Explicit examples: simple observables

Thanks to the central limit theorem (CLT), the simplest choice of
Y(X)
is just the summation of
N
independent and identical random samples. We will first check proposal with three explicit distributions: the Gaussian distribution, the exponential distribution and the Poisson distribution and then give a general proof for general cases. Readers who are bored with these examples can jump into the general proof directly.

Let
Y
to be a summation of
N
independent and identical random variables, i.e.

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i},\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}^{N}X_{i}X_{j}.

(579)

\bullet

Gaussian distribution

The PDF of Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu,t^{2})
is

\displaystyle P(x)=\frac{1}{t\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{x-\mu}{t})^{2}}.

(580)

Given (
580
) one can straightforwardly compute the averaged quantities

\displaystyle\langle X_{i}\rangle=\mu,\quad\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle=e^{-\frac{k_{i}^{2}t^{2}}{2}+\text{i}k_{i}\mu},\quad\langle Y^{2}\rangle=Nt^{2}+N^{2}\mu^{2},

(581)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle Ye^{\sum_{i}\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\sum_{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle}=\sum_{i}\frac{\langle x_{i}e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}\equiv\sum_{i}k_{i}[1],\quad k_{i}[1]=(u+\text{i}k_{i}t^{2}).

(582)

where we have defined

\displaystyle k_{i}[n]=\frac{\langle x^{n}_{i}e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}.

(583)

Let us introduce another convenient quantity

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{i}[n]^{m}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int dk_{i}\frac{e^{-\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}}{P(X_{i})}\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle k_{i}[n]^{m},

(584)

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{i}[1]}=X_{i},\quad\widehat{k_{i}[1]^{2}}=X_{i}^{2}-t^{2},\quad\dots

(585)

then the half-wormhole can be written as

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\widehat{k_{i}[1]^{2}}+\sum_{i\neq j}\widehat{k_{i}[1]}\widehat{k_{j}[1]}=\sum_{i}(X_{i}^{2}-t^{2})+\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j},

(586)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{2}-Nt^{2}

(587)

Substituting into (
20
) one can computed the error and the ration
\rho
directly

Error

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{2}-\left(Nt^{2}+N^{2}\mu^{2}-N^{2}\mu^{2}+Y^{2}-Nt^{2}\right)

(588)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(589)

The proposal is exact as expected.

\bullet

Exponential distribution

The PDF of exponential distribution is given by

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(x)=\begin{cases}\lambda e^{-\lambda x},\quad&x\geq 0,\\
0,&x<0,\end{cases}

(590)

and the moments are given by

\displaystyle\langle x^{n}\rangle=\frac{n!}{\lambda^{n}}.

(591)

The relevant averaged quantities are

\displaystyle\langle X_{i}\rangle=\frac{1}{\lambda},\quad\langle e^{\text{i}kX_{i}}\rangle=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-\text{i}k},\quad\langle Y^{2}\rangle=\frac{N(N+1)}{\lambda^{2}}.

(592)

From the example of Gaussian distribution we have shown that to compute
\Phi
(
587
) and the error (
588
) we only need to compute

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{i}[1]}=X_{i},\quad\widehat{k_{i}[1]^{2}}=\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{2},

(593)

which lead to

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}\left(\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\right),\quad\rho\approx\frac{\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle}{\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N^{3}}.

(594)

So the proposal is correct.

\bullet

Poisson distribution

Next let us examine the proposal for a discrete probability distribution: the Poisson distribution. The PDF is

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(k)=\frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{k}}{k!},\quad k=0,1,2,\dots

(595)

and the moments are given by

\displaystyle\langle x^{n}\rangle=B_{n}(\lambda),

(596)

where
B_{n}(\lambda)
is the Bell polynomial.
The relevant averaged quantities can be easily computed

\displaystyle\langle X_{i}\rangle={\lambda},\quad\langle e^{\text{i}kX_{i}}\rangle=e^{-\lambda+e^{\text{i}k}\lambda},\quad\langle Y^{2}\rangle=N\lambda+N^{2}\lambda^{2},

(597)

\displaystyle k_{i}[1]=e^{\text{i}k_{i}}\lambda.

(598)

The computation of
\widehat{k_{i}[1]}
and
\widehat{k_{i}[1]^{2}}
is a little subtle and needs some explanation. According to the definition (
584
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{i}[1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2\pi P(X_{i})}\int dk_{i}{e^{-\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}}{}\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle e^{\text{i}k_{i}},

(599)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{P(X_{i})}P(X_{i}-1)=X_{i},

(600)

where in the second line we have used the fact that the inverse Fourier transformation of the characteristic function is the PDF

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int dk_{i}{e^{-\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}}{}\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle=P(X_{i}).

(601)

Similarly one can derive

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{i}[1]^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\frac{P(X_{i}-2)}{P(X_{i})}=X_{i}(X_{i}-1).

(602)

Then using (
588
) we can obtain the error

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}(X_{i}-\lambda),\quad\rho\approx\frac{\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle}{\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N^{3}},

(603)

so the proposal is correct while the effective parameter is
\lambda N
instead of
N
.

Appendix B
Explicite Examples: composite observables

Gaussian distribution

We still start from the simple model

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}^{2},\quad\langle X_{i}^{2}\rangle=t^{2},\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}.

(604)

It is easy to evaluate

\displaystyle\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle=\exp(-\sum_{i}\frac{k_{i}^{2}t^{2}}{2}),

(605)

\displaystyle\langle Ye^{\text{i}\sum_{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle/\langle e^{\text{i}\sum_{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle=\sum_{i}(-k_{i}^{2}t^{4}+t^{2}).

(606)

Then we find

\displaystyle\Phi=\sum_{i}\left(X_{i}^{4}-4t^{2}X_{i}^{2}+2t^{4}\right)+\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2},

(607)

and the error is given by

\displaystyle\text{Error}=4Nt^{4}-4t^{2}\sum_{i}X_{i}^{2}.

(608)

From this we can easily find that the proposal is correct.

Let us consider

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i}X_{i}^{3},\quad\langle X_{i}^{3}\rangle=0,

(609)

\displaystyle Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}X_{i}^{3}X_{j}^{3},\quad\langle Y^{2}\rangle=15Nt^{6}.

(610)

Then we find can evaluate

\displaystyle\langle Ye^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle/\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle=\sum_{i}\text{i}k_{i}t^{4}(3-k_{i}^{2}t^{2}),

(611)

and

\displaystyle\Phi=\sum_{i}(X_{i}^{6}-9t^{2}X_{i}^{4}+18t^{4}X_{i}^{2}-6t^{6})+\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{3}X_{j}^{3}.

(612)

So that the error is given by

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}(9t^{2}X_{i}^{4}-18t^{4}X_{i}^{2}-9t^{6}),

(613)

the leading order of
\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle
is

\displaystyle 0N^{2}.

(614)

Let us consider

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i}X_{i}^{4},\quad\langle X_{i}^{4}\rangle=3t^{4},\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}X_{i}^{4}X_{j}^{4}

(615)

then we find can evaluate

\displaystyle\langle Ye^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle/\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle=\sum_{i}t^{4}(k_{i}^{4}t^{4}-6k_{i}^{2}t^{2}+3)

(616)

and

\displaystyle\Phi=8\sum_{i}(3t^{8}-12t^{6}X_{i}^{2}+9t^{3}X_{i}^{4}-2t^{2}X_{i}^{6})+Y^{2}

(617)

such that the error is

\displaystyle\text{Error}=-8\sum_{i}(15t^{8}-12t^{6}X_{i}^{2}+9t^{3}X_{i}^{4}-2t^{2}X_{i}^{6}).

(618)

We can also find the leading order of
\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle
is zero.

In general let us consider

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i}e^{mX_{i}},\quad\langle e^{mX_{i}}\rangle=e^{\frac{m^{2}t^{2}}{2}},\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}e^{mX_{i}}e^{mX_{j}},

(619)

and

\displaystyle\Phi=\sum_{i}(e^{m(-mt^{2}+2X_{i})}-e^{2mX_{i}})+Y^{2},

(620)

such that

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}(e^{-m^{2}t^{2}}-1)(e^{2m^{2}t^{2}}-e^{2mX_{i}}),

(621)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=Ne^{4m^{2}t^{2}}(e^{4m^{2}t^{2}}-1)(e^{-m^{2}t^{2}}-1)^{2},

(622)

which is sub-dominant comparing to
\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2}
.

Exponential distribution

First let us choose

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i}X_{i}^{2},\quad\langle X_{i}^{2}\rangle=\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}},\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}.

(623)

Correspondingly we find

\displaystyle\frac{\langle Ye^{\text{i}kX_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}kX_{i}}\rangle}=-\frac{2}{(k+\text{i}\lambda)^{2}}

(624)

and

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{5}{6}\sum_{i}X_{i}^{4}+\sum_{i,j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}.

(625)

So the error is given by

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}\left(\frac{5X_{i}^{4}}{6}-\frac{20}{\lambda^{4}}\right),

(626)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=\frac{25N}{36\lambda^{8}}(8!-4!^{2}).

(627)

In general, let us consider

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i}e^{\beta X_{i}},\quad\langle e^{\beta X_{i}}\rangle=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda-\beta},\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}e^{\beta X_{i}}e^{\beta X_{j}}.

(628)

Then we can obtain

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left(e^{\beta X_{i}}(1-e^{\beta X_{i}}+\beta X_{i})\right)+\sum_{i,j}e^{\beta X_{i}}e^{\beta X_{j}}.

(629)

So the error is

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}\left(e^{2\beta X_{i}}-e^{\beta X_{i}}(1+\beta X_{i})\right)-\frac{N\beta^{2}\lambda}{(\beta-\lambda)^{2}(\lambda-2\beta)},

(630)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=N\left(\frac{2\beta^{4}(3\lambda-8\beta)}{(2\beta-\lambda)^{3}(4\beta-\lambda)(\lambda-3\beta)}-\frac{\lambda^{2}\beta^{4}}{(\lambda-\beta)^{4}(\lambda-2\beta)^{2}}\right),

(631)

given that the condition

\displaystyle\beta<\frac{\lambda}{4},

(632)

to ensure that all the integrals to be convergent.

Poisson distribution

Let us choose

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i}X_{i}^{2},\quad\langle X_{i}^{2}\rangle=\lambda^{2}+\lambda,\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}

(633)

Then we find

\displaystyle\frac{\langle Ye^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle}=\sum_{i}\lambda e^{\text{i}k_{i}}(1+\lambda e^{\text{i}k_{i}})

(634)

and

\displaystyle\Phi=\sum_{i}\left(X_{i}(-3+6X_{i}-4X_{i}^{2})\right)+\sum_{i,j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}.

(635)

Therefore the error is

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}\left(X_{i}(3-6X_{i}+4X_{i}^{2})-\lambda(1+6\lambda+4\lambda^{2})\right)

(636)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=N\lambda(24\lambda(\lambda(6\lambda(\lambda+4)+23)+4)+1)

(637)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle}{\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N^{3}}

(638)

In general let us consider

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i}e^{\beta X_{i}},\quad\langle e^{\beta X_{i}}\rangle=\exp\lambda(e^{\beta}-1),\quad Y^{2}=\sum_{i,j}e^{\beta X_{i}}e^{\beta X_{j}}.

(639)

Then we find

\displaystyle\frac{\langle Ye^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}\rangle}=e^{\left(e^{\beta}-1\right)e^{\text{i}k_{i}}\lambda}

(640)

and

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}(\left(2e^{\beta}-1\right)^{X_{i}}-e^{2\beta X_{i}})+Y^{2}.

(641)

Therefore the error is

\displaystyle\text{Error}=\sum_{i}\left(-\left(2e^{\beta}-1\right)^{X_{i}}+e^{2\beta X_{i}}-e^{-2\lambda}\left(e^{e^{2\beta}\lambda+\lambda}-e^{2e^{\beta}\lambda}\right)\right)

(642)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=-e^{-4\lambda}N\left(e^{4e^{\beta}\lambda}-2e^{\left(e^{\beta}+1\right)^{2}\lambda}+e^{2\left(e^{2\beta}+1\right)\lambda}-e^{\left(e^{4\beta}+3\right)\lambda}-e^{4\left(e^{\beta}\left(e^{\beta}-1\right)+1\right)\lambda}+2e^{\left(e^{2\beta}\left(2e^{\beta}-1\right)+3\right)\lambda}\right)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle}{\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{N^{3}}.

(644)

Appendix C
Explicte Examples: generalized statistical models

The exponential distribution

First let us consider the exponential distribution. It is straightforward to derive

\displaystyle\langle Y\rangle=\langle\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j}\rangle=N(N-1)\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}},

(645)

\displaystyle\langle Y^{2}\rangle=\frac{2N(N-1)(2N-1)+N^{2}(N-1)^{2}}{\lambda^{4}}

(646)

and

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j\neq p\neq q}X_{i}X_{j}X_{p}X_{q}+4\sum_{i\neq j\neq p}\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{2}X_{j}X_{p}+2\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{X_{i}^{2}}{2}\frac{X_{j}^{2}}{2}

(647)

in particular as a consistency check

\displaystyle\langle\Phi\rangle=N^{2}(N-1)^{2}\frac{1}{\lambda^{4}}.

(648)

Therefore the error is

\displaystyle\text{Error}=2\sum_{i\neq j\neq p}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}X_{p}+\frac{3}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}-\langle Y^{2}\rangle,

(649)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle\sim(4*2*2+16-4*2*4)N^{6}+\#N^{5}=\#N^{5}/\lambda^{8}.

(650)

Poisson distribution

First we compute

\displaystyle\langle\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j}\rangle=N(N-1)\lambda,

(651)

\displaystyle\langle Y^{2}\rangle=2N(N-1)(1+2(N-1)\lambda)\lambda^{2}+N^{2}(N-1)^{2}\lambda^{2},

(652)

and

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j\neq p\neq q}X_{i}X_{j}X_{p}X_{q}+4\sum_{i\neq j\neq p}(X_{i}^{2}-X_{i})X_{j}X_{p}+2\sum_{i\neq j}(X_{i}^{2}-X_{i})(X_{j}^{2}-X_{j}).

(653)

One can check that

\displaystyle\langle\Phi\rangle=N^{2}(N-1)^{2}\lambda^{2}.

(654)

Therefore the error is

\displaystyle\text{Error}=-\langle Y^{2}\rangle+4\sum_{i\neq j\neq p}X_{i}X_{j}X_{p}+4\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}-2\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j}

(655)

and it is easy to check (note that only the first two term in (
655
) will contribute)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle\sim 0N^{6}+\#N^{5}.

(656)

Appendix D
Exact evaluation of
\langle z^{4}\rangle
for Brownian SYK at large T

We can only consider one
f
term in the logarithm, we expect it gives the non-trivial contribution in the large
T
limit. For instance we have

\displaystyle e^{\frac{\text{i}}{2}\sqrt{(\partial_{G_{14}}+\partial_{G_{23}})^{2}+(\partial_{G_{13}}-\partial_{G_{24}})^{2}+(\partial_{G_{12}}+\partial_{G_{34}})^{2}}}e^{2NTJ/q\sum_{a,b}s_{ab}G_{ab}^{q}}

(657)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\frac{\text{i}}{2}\right)^{m}}{m!}\left[(\partial_{G_{14}}+\partial_{G_{23}})^{2}+(\partial_{G_{13}}-\partial_{G_{24}})^{2}+(\partial_{G_{12}}+\partial_{G_{34}})^{2}\right]^{m/2}e^{2NTJ/q\sum_{a,b}s_{ab}G_{ab}^{q}}

(658)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\frac{\text{i}}{2}\right)^{m}}{m!}\sum_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\frac{\frac{m}{2}!}{\frac{k_{1}}{2}!\frac{k_{2}}{2}!\frac{k_{3}}{2}!}(\partial_{G_{14}}+\partial_{G_{23}})^{k_{1}}(\partial_{G_{13}}-\partial_{G_{24}})^{k_{2}}(\partial_{G_{12}}+\partial_{G_{34}})^{k_{3}}e^{\frac{2NTJ}{q}\sum_{a,b}s_{ab}G_{ab}^{q}},

(659)

and

\displaystyle(\partial_{G_{14}}+\partial_{G_{23}})^{k_{1}}e^{-2NTJ/q\left(G_{14}^{q}+G_{23}^{q}\right)}=\sum_{l_{1}+l_{2}=k_{1}}\frac{k_{1}!}{l_{1}!l_{2}!}\partial_{G_{14}}^{l_{1}}\partial_{G_{23}}^{l_{2}}e^{-(2NTJ/q)G_{14}^{q}}e^{-(2NTJ/q)G_{23}^{q}}

(660)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1}+l_{2}=k_{1}}\left(-\frac{2NTJ}{q}\right)^{k_{1}/q}\frac{k_{1}!}{(l_{1}/q)!(l_{2}/q)!},

(661)

where now
k_{1}
is a multiple of
q
. Then (
657
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\text{i}/2)^{m}}{m!}\sum_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\frac{(m/2)!}{(k_{1}/2)!(k_{2}/2)!(k_{3}/2)!}\sum_{l_{1}+l_{2}=k_{1}}\left(-\frac{2NTJ}{q}\right)^{k_{1}/q}\frac{k_{1}!}{(l_{1}/q)!(l_{2}/q)!}

(662)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=k_{2}}\left(\text{i}^{q}\frac{2NTJ}{q}\right)^{k_{2}/q}\frac{k_{2}!}{(r_{1}/q)!(r_{2}/q)!}\sum_{s_{1}+s_{2}=k_{3}}\left(\text{i}^{q}\frac{2NTJ}{q}\right)^{k_{3}/q}\frac{k_{3}!}{(s_{1}/q)!(s_{2}/q)!},

(663)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\frac{\text{i}}{2})^{m}}{m!}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=m}\frac{\frac{m}{2}!}{\frac{k_{1}}{2}!\frac{k_{2}}{2}!\frac{k_{3}}{2}!}\left(-\frac{4NTJ}{q}\right)^{\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}}{q}}\frac{k_{1}!k_{2}!k_{3}!}{\frac{k_{1}}{q}!\frac{k_{2}}{q}!\frac{k_{3}}{q}!}(-\text{i}^{q})^{\frac{k_{2}+k_{3}}{q}}

(664)

where
m
is also a multiple of
q
. To evaluate it we can take an approximation when
m>q

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=m}(-\text{i}^{q})^{\frac{k_{2}+k_{3}}{q}}\frac{k_{1}!k_{2}!k_{3}!}{m!}\frac{\frac{m}{2}!}{\frac{k_{1}}{2}!\frac{k_{2}}{2}!\frac{k_{3}}{2}!}\frac{\frac{m}{q}!}{\frac{k_{1}}{q}!\frac{k_{2}}{q}!\frac{k_{3}}{q}!}\cong\begin{cases}3\,,\qquad q=4k+2\\
3\,,\qquad q=4k,\quad m/q\text{ is even}\\
-1\,,~\quad q=4k,\quad m/q\text{ is odd}\end{cases}\

(665)

then up to a constant (
657
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{cases}3e^{\frac{4NTJ}{q2^{q}}},\hskip 128.0374ptq=4k+2\\
3\cosh\left(\frac{4NTJ}{q2^{q}}\right)+\sinh\left(\frac{4NTJ}{q2^{q}}\right),\quad q=4k.\end{cases}

(666)

Appendix E
Lefschetz Thimbles

In this appendix, we review the method of Lefschetz thimble
Witten:2010cx
.
Suppose we would like to evaluate the integral

\displaystyle Z=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{R}}}dx^{i}e^{S}\,,

(667)

where the integration contour is
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then we complexify the manifold on which the integration is done to
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
. If we choose
\Re(S)
to be the Morse function. The saddle points of the integral are the critical points of the Morse function. Around each critical point on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
we introduce a set of local coordinates
\{z_{i}\}
. The Morse flow is determined by the flow equations

\displaystyle\frac{dz^{i}}{dt}=-g^{i\bar{j}}\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial\bar{z}^{j}},\quad\frac{d\bar{z}^{i}}{dt}=-g^{i\bar{j}}\frac{\partial S}{\partial z^{j}}\ .

(668)

We find

\displaystyle\frac{d(S-\bar{S})}{dt}=\frac{\partial S}{\partial z^{i}}\frac{dz^{i}}{dt}-\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial\bar{z}^{i}}\frac{d\bar{z}^{i}}{dt}=0\,,

(669)

which implies that the imaginary part of
S
is a constant along the flow. Each of the critical points is associated with a pair of flows, the thimble and the anti-thimble. The thimble is the “stable” direction such that the Morse function
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{R}}
decays along the thimble and the integral of
\exp(S)
along the thimble converges. On the contrary, the anti-thimble is the “unstable” direction. Explicitly the boundary conditions for a particular critical point
p_{\sigma}
are

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to-\infty}z(t)=p_{\sigma},\quad\text{for thimbles},

(670)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}z(t)=p_{\sigma},\quad\text{for anti-thimbles}.

(671)

The main statement in
Witten:2010cx
that we will use repeatedly is that the integral can be approximated by a weighted sum over integrals along the thimbles of each critical point

\displaystyle Z=\sum_{i}n_{i}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{i}}dt\,e^{S[t]}\,,

(672)

where
i
runs over all the critical points,
\mathcal{J}_{i}
is the Lefschetz thimble attaching to the
i^{\text{th}}
critical point, and the weight
n_{i}
is given by the intersection number between the anti-thimble and the original integration contour
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
.

E.1
some examples

To illustrate how this works, we first go through some simple examples.

E.1.1
The Gaussian function

Let us consider a simple example with

\displaystyle S=-x^{2}/2+\sigma x\ .

(673)

The integral can be regarded as a zero-dimension theory with quadratic interaction and a complex source
\sigma
. The only critical point is at
x=\sigma=a+\text{i}b
. The flow equation is

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x}{\text{d}t}=\bar{x}-(a-\text{i}b)\ .

(674)

Expressing
x=x_{1}+\text{i}x_{2}
, we get the following equations

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x_{1}}{\text{d}t}=x_{1}-a,\quad\frac{\text{d}x_{2}}{\text{d}t}=b-x_{2}\ .

(675)

The general solution can be easily solved

\displaystyle x_{1}=a+c_{1}e^{t},\quad x_{2}=b+c_{2}e^{-t},

(676)

where
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
are two undetermined constants. The boundary conditions for the thimble is

\displaystyle(x_{1},x_{2})\to(a,b),\quad t\to-\infty,

(677)

while for the anti-thimble we have

\displaystyle(x_{1},x_{2})\to(a,b),\quad t\to+\infty,

(678)

where
(a,b)
is the critical point. Then with these boundary conditions we can get the equations for the thimble and the anti-thimble respectively

\displaystyle x_{2}=b,

(679)

\displaystyle x_{1}=a.

(680)

We plot the thimble and the anti-thimble in this case in Figure
14
, where for simplicity we let
\sigma=1+\text{i}
.

We can also compare the saddle point solution with the exact result. The integral can evaluated as

\displaystyle\int\text{d}xe^{-x^{2}/2+\sigma x}=\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\sigma^{2}/2}.

(681)

While the saddle point solution gives

\displaystyle e^{\sigma^{2}/2},

(682)

with the one-loop correction
\sqrt{2\pi}
the saddle point analysis recovers the exact result.

Figure 14
:
The red line denotes the thimble and the blue line denotes the anti-thimble. The anti-thimble intersects with the real line, so this saddle point contributes

E.1.2
The Airy function

A slightly less trivial example is the Airy action

\displaystyle Z_{\lambda}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}xe^{S}\,,\quad S=\text{i}\lambda\left(\frac{x^{3}}{3}-x\right)\ .

(683)

It is not hard to find that for real
\lambda
there are three “convergent” regions, namely
\Re(S)<\infty
, on the complex
x
-plane:

\displaystyle x=re^{\text{i}\theta},\quad\frac{2n\pi}{3}\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{3}+\frac{2n\pi}{3},\quad n=0,1,2.

(684)

In each convergent region, the Airy integrand is exponentially small. As we vary
\lambda
to complex values, we should deform the integration contour of
x
accordingly so that the integral remains converge. This gives an analytic continuation of
Z_{\lambda}
. The two critical points are located at
x=\pm 1
. The values of saddle points are

\displaystyle S_{\pm}=\mp\frac{2\text{i}\lambda}{3}.

(685)

Since along the (anti-)thimbles, the imaginary part of
S
is a constant and

\displaystyle\text{Im}(S_{\pm})=\mp\frac{2\text{Re}(\lambda)}{3}.

(686)

Therefore the two (anti-)thimbles associated with the two critical points will not intersect except for the case of
\text{Re}(\lambda)=0
. The thimble which connects critical points is called the Stoke ray. Using the Lefschetz thimbles
\mathcal{J}_{\pm}
, we can rewrite the integral as

\displaystyle Z_{\lambda}=n_{+}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{+}}\exp S+n_{-}\int_{\mathcal{J}_{-}}\exp S.

(687)

To solve the thimbles, let us take
\lambda=1
, then the flow equations are

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x}{\text{d}t}=\text{i}(\bar{x}^{2}-1).

(688)

Expressing
x=x_{1}+\text{i}x_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x_{1}}{\text{d}t}=2x_{1}x_{2},\quad\frac{\text{d}x_{2}}{\text{d}t}=x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}-1.

(689)

We plot the anti-thimbles in Fig.
15

Figure 15
:
The red line denotes the anti-thimble of
x=-1
and the Green line denotes the anti-thimble of
x=1
.The grey regions are the convergent regions.

Therefore for
\lambda=1
both of the saddle points contribute. This result is expected since that the two critical points are already located on the real line.

The problem we met in the main text is better illustrated by the following toy model

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\text{d}x\exp S,\quad S=\text{i}\left(\frac{x^{3}}{3}+x\right)\ .

(690)

The integral is convergent and can be expressed by the Airy function

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}=\frac{2\pi\text{Ai}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{3}}\right)}{\sqrt[3]{3}}=0.83\ .

(691)

We now try to compute the integral with saddle point approximation, where the saddle points are located at
x=\pm\text{i}
.
The saddle point value, plus the 1-loop correction, of the integral at these two saddle points,
\tilde{Z}_{\pm}
are the same, and the sum of them is larger than the exact evaluation of the integral

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{+}=\tilde{Z}_{-}=0.733,\quad\tilde{Z}_{+}+\tilde{Z}_{-}>\tilde{Z}.

(692)

This is exactly the situation we are encountering.
In this toy model, it is easy to show that the anti-thimble associated with the saddle point
x=-\text{i}
does not intersect with the real axis, Figure.
16
, so the saddle point
x=-\text{i}
does not contribute to the integral.

Figure 16
:
The red line is the anti-thimble of
x=\text{i}
which intersects with the real line while the Green line is the anti-thimble of
x=-\text{i}
which does not intersect with the real line. The grey regions are the convergent regions.

E.2
Multi-variable cases

Let us consider another example with two variables

\displaystyle Z=\int\text{d}\sigma\frac{\text{d}g}{2\pi}e^{S}\,,\quad S=\log\sigma-\text{i}\sigma g-\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\ .

(693)

The integral can be done directly to get

\displaystyle Z=0\ .

(694)

There are two saddle points

\displaystyle g_{\pm}=\pm\text{i},\quad\sigma_{\pm}=\mp 1\ .

(695)

with saddle point contributions to the integral (on-shell action)

\displaystyle Z_{\pm}=\mp\frac{1}{\sqrt{e}},\quad Z_{-}+Z_{+}=0\ ,

(696)

Matching this with the exact solution suggests that
n_{\pm}=1
. Note that
\sigma_{\pm}=\mp 1
are already on the real line so corresponding anti-thimbles always intersect with the original contour. The flow equations are

\displaystyle\frac{d\sigma}{dt}=-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}}-\text{i}\bar{g},\quad\frac{\text{d}g}{dt}=-\text{i}\bar{\sigma}+\bar{g}.

(697)

Expressing
\sigma=\sigma_{1}+\text{i}\sigma_{2}
and
g=g_{1}+\text{i}g_{2}
we obtain the following differential equations

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}\sigma_{1}}{\text{d}t}+g_{2}+\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}}=0,\quad\frac{\text{d}\sigma_{2}}{\text{d}t}+g_{1}+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}}=0,

(698)

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}g_{1}}{\text{d}t}+\sigma_{2}-g_{1}=0,\quad\frac{\text{d}g_{2}}{\text{d}t}+\sigma_{1}+g_{2}=0.

(699)

We find that indeed these two saddles should both be included. We plot the
g
-plane of the anti-thimbles in Fig.
17
.

Figure 17
:
Since
\sigma_{\pm}
are already on the real line here we only plot the
g
-plane of the anti-thimbles. Clearly both of these two anti-thimbles intersect with the real axis so these two saddle points both contribute to the integral.

Note that this example is special case of (
172
) with
q=2
.

Flow equations in real coordinates

Sometimes it more convenient to use real form of the flow equations (
668
). We start with the relations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S}{\partial z}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial S}{\partial x}+\frac{1}{2\text{i}}\frac{\partial S}{\partial y},

(700)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial\bar{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial x}-\frac{1}{2\text{i}}\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial y},

(701)

where

\displaystyle z=x+\text{i}y.

(702)

Then we evaluate the equation as

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}z}{\text{d}t}+\frac{\text{d}\bar{z}}{\text{d}t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial S}{\partial z}-\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial\bar{z}}=-\frac{\partial\text{Re}(S)}{\partial x}-\frac{\partial\text{Im}(S)}{\partial y},

(703)

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}z}{\text{d}t}-\frac{\text{d}\bar{z}}{\text{d}t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial S}{\partial z}-\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial\bar{z}}=-\text{i}\frac{\partial\text{Re}(S)}{\partial y}+\text{i}\frac{\partial\text{Im}(S)}{\partial x},

(704)

where we work in the flat space.
Recall the Cauchy-Riemann equation we get

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x}{\text{d}t}=-\frac{\partial\text{Re}(S)}{\partial x},\quad\frac{\text{d}y}{\text{d}t}=-\frac{\partial\text{Re}(S)}{\partial y}.

(705)

To illustrate it we consider a special case in the Airy function

\displaystyle S=\text{i}\left(\frac{x^{3}}{3}+x\right).

(706)

In the complex plane its conjugate is

\displaystyle\bar{S}=-\text{i}\left(\frac{\bar{x}^{3}}{3}+\bar{x}\right),

(707)

and we can define the components

\displaystyle x=x_{1}+\text{i}x_{2},\quad\bar{x}=x_{1}-\text{i}x_{2}.

(708)

The flow equation in complex coordinates becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x}{\text{d}t}=-\frac{\partial\bar{S}}{\partial\bar{x}}=\text{i}(\bar{x}^{2}+1),

(709)

which leads to the equations in real coordinates

\displaystyle\frac{\text{d}x_{1}}{\text{d}t}=2x_{1}x_{2},\quad\frac{\text{d}x_{2}}{\text{d}t}=x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}+1.

(710)

On the other hand we can get the equations with the real part of
S
:

\displaystyle\text{Re}(S)=-x_{2}-x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+\frac{x_{2}^{3}}{3}.

(711)

From the equations (
705
) we can recover the two flow equations (
710
).

Appendix F
Averaged models

In this section, before talking about higher dimensional model we detour the main topic a little bit and consider the averaged theory in general. Let us consider a real scalar field with a chemical potential. The partition function of the theory is

\displaystyle Z=\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,\partial_{\mu}\phi(x)\partial^{\mu}\phi(x)+J(x)\phi(x)\right)\,

(712)

where
J(x)
is a random source drawn from some probability distribution. If the random coupling
J
does not depends on the spacetime i.e.

\displaystyle\langle J^{n}\rangle_{\text{connected}}=\kappa_{n}\,\quad

(713)

where
\kappa_{n}
is the
n
-th cumulants of the probability distribution. This situation is like the regular SYK theory. To take the ensemble average we can expand the exponential as

\displaystyle\langle Z\rangle_{J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}\right)\sum_{n}\frac{\langle J^{n}\rangle}{n!}\left(\int dx\,\phi(x)\right)^{n}

(714)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}\right)\text{MGF}\left(\int dx\phi(x)\right)

(715)

where
MGF
is the moment generating function of
J
. If
J
is Gaussian
{\cal N}(0,t^{2})
then

\displaystyle\text{MGF}\left(\int dx\phi(x)\right)=\exp\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2}\int dx\,dy\,\phi(x)\phi(y)\right),

(716)

\displaystyle\langle Z\rangle_{J}=\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\int dx\,dy\,\phi(x)\phi(y)\right)\,

(717)

which is similar to SYK model that after the Gaussian average, bi-local interaction is generated. For the general distribution (
713
), the resulting averaged action is

\displaystyle\langle Z\rangle_{J}=\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}+\sum_{n}\frac{\kappa_{n}}{n!}[\int dx\phi(x)]^{n}\right)\,,

(718)

so multi-local interactions will be generated. In particular, if the distribution is Poisson we have

\displaystyle\langle Z\rangle_{J}=\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}+e^{\lambda\int dx\phi(x)}-1\right)\,,

(719)

which is a highly non-local theory.

Alternatively we can require the random coupling
J
to depend on the spacetime i.e.

\displaystyle\langle\prod^{n}_{i}J(x_{i})\rangle=\prod_{i=1,j=i}^{n}\delta(x_{i}-x_{j})\kappa_{n}\,.

(720)

This situation is like the Brownian SYK theory
Saad:2018bqo
. Now we can take the ensemble average to get

\displaystyle\langle Z\rangle_{J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}\right)\sum_{n}\frac{\langle J^{n}\rangle}{n!}\left(\int dx\,\phi(x)\right)^{n}

(721)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}\right)\sum_{n}\frac{\kappa_{n}}{n!}\left(\int dx\,\phi^{n}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,{\cal L}_{0}\right)\int dx\,\text{MGF}(\phi(x)).

(722)

If
J
is Gaussian
{\cal N}(0,t^{2})
then we have

\displaystyle\langle Z\rangle_{J}=\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,({\cal L}_{0}+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\phi^{2}(x))\right).

(723)

and if
J
is Poisson then we simply get

\displaystyle\langle Z\rangle_{J}=\int{\cal D}\Phi\exp\left(-\int dx\,[{\cal L}_{0}+\lambda(e^{\phi(x)}-1)]\right),

(724)

which also coincides with results in
Peng:2020rno
.

Appendix G
Computations in large N

CLT with
\mu=0

In the appendix, we will consider the half-wormhole conjecture for statistical model in a more systematic way.
The error functions we care about are

Error

\displaystyle=Y^{2}-\langle Y^{2}\rangle+\langle Y\rangle^{2}-\Phi,

(725)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\left(Y^{2}-\langle Y^{2}\rangle+\langle Y\rangle^{2}-\Phi\right)^{2}\rangle,

(726)

where
\Phi
is defined in (
63
). And if the approximation is proper we must have

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}\rangle=0,\quad\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle/\langle Y^{4}\rangle\ll 1.

(727)

The first requirement leads to

\displaystyle\langle\Phi\rangle=\langle Y\rangle^{2}

(728)

which is proved around (
63
), then we have

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=\langle\left(Y^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}-\Phi\right)^{2}\rangle-\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2}.

(729)

In the following we’ll give one type of function and try to explore the feasibility of the proposal, i.e. we compute the two error functions and check the relations (
727
) respectively.

First we consider the function
Y
consisting of
N
identical and independent variables
X_{i}
as

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j},

(730)

and for simplicity each combination only appears once since
X_{i}
’s commute with each other. The properties for
X_{i}
in this section are set as

\displaystyle\langle X\rangle=0,\quad\langle X^{2}\rangle=t^{2},

(731)

in the next section we’ll consider the distribution with a non-zero mean.
But actually for any distribution the property (
731
) can always be satisfied since we can always take the subtraction

\displaystyle\tilde{X}=X-\langle X\rangle.

(732)

therefore our discussion may be applied into the case with any distribution.

Obviously the first requirement in (
727
) is satisfied then we consider the computation for the second requirement

\displaystyle Y^{2}=\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}+2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}X^{2}_{i}X_{j}X_{k}+6\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq l}X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}X_{l},

(733)

where the first sum contains
p
different terms

\displaystyle p={N\choose 2},

(734)

while we have
2p(N-2)/2
for the second sum and
p(p-2(N-2)-1)/6
for the third one. For the
\Phi
in the approximation we have

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N}}\int d\vec{k}\frac{e^{-\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{X}}}{P(\vec{X})}\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle\left(\frac{\langle Ye^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}\right)^{2},

(735)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N}}\int d\vec{k}\frac{e^{-\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{X}}}{P(\vec{X})}\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\langle x_{i}x_{j}e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}\right)^{2},

(736)

the computation is similar to that in
Y^{2}
so that we can split the
\Phi
function into three parts according to the three sums in (
733
)

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{N}}\int d\vec{k}\frac{e^{-\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{X}}}{P(\vec{X})}\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{\langle x_{i}x_{j}e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}\frac{\langle x_{i}x_{j}e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle},

(737)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{1}{\left(2\pi\right)^{2}}\int dk_{i}dk_{j}\frac{e^{-\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}e^{-\text{i}k_{j}X_{j}}}{P(X_{i})P(X_{j})}\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle\langle e^{\text{i}k_{j}x_{j}}\rangle\left(\frac{\langle x_{i}e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\langle x_{j}e^{\text{i}k_{j}x_{j}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}k_{j}x_{j}}\rangle}\right)^{2},

(738)

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{N}}\int d\vec{k}\frac{e^{-\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{X}}}{P(\vec{X})}\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\frac{\langle x_{i}x_{j}e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}\frac{\langle x_{i}x_{k}e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle},

(739)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}X_{j}X_{k}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int dk_{i}\frac{e^{-\text{i}k_{i}X_{i}}}{P(X_{i})}\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle\left(\frac{\langle x_{i}e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}k_{i}x_{i}}\rangle}\right)^{2},

(740)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{(2\pi)^{N}}\int d\vec{k}\frac{e^{-\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{X}}}{P(\vec{X})}\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq l}\frac{\langle x_{i}x_{j}e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}\frac{\langle x_{k}x_{l}e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}\vec{k}\vec{x}}\rangle},

(741)

\displaystyle=6\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq l}X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}X_{l}.

(742)

For simplicity we can define the function below

\displaystyle\phi_{i}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int dk\frac{e^{-\text{i}kX_{i}}}{P(X_{i})}\langle e^{\text{i}kx}\rangle\left(\frac{\langle xe^{\text{i}kx}\rangle}{\langle e^{\text{i}kx}\rangle}\right)^{2},

(743)

so that the
\Phi
functions can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}=\sum_{i\neq j}\phi_{i}\phi_{j},

(744)

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}=2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\phi_{i}X_{j}X_{k}.

(745)

And note that the
\phi
function has the property

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{i}\rangle=\langle X_{i}\rangle^{2},

(746)

which is useful in the later computation, where
X_{i}
’s are identical.

Then following the procedure we have

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\left(Y^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}-\Phi\right)^{2}\rangle-\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2},

(747)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\left(X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}-\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\right)+2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\left(X^{2}_{i}-\phi_{i}\right)X_{j}X_{k}\right)^{2}\right\rangle-p^{2}\langle X^{2}\rangle^{4},

(748)

where the last sum cancels out and
p
is defined in (
734
). The cross terms between different sums are zero due to the zero mean of
X_{i}
, therefore we can only consider the squares of each sum

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=\left\langle\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\left(X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}-\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\right)\right)^{2}+\left(2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\left(X^{2}_{i}-\phi_{i}\right)X_{j}X_{k}\right)^{2}\right\rangle-p^{2}\langle X^{2}\rangle^{4}.

(749)

The numbers of the diagonal terms in the two sums in the first average are

\displaystyle p,\quad 4p(N-2),

(750)

while the non-diagonal terms in the first sum have

\displaystyle p(p-2(N-2)-1)\quad\text{ for }\quad\langle X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}X_{k}^{2}X_{l}^{2}\rangle

(751)

\displaystyle 2p(N-2)\quad\text{ for }\quad\langle X_{i}^{4}X_{j}^{2}X_{k}^{2}\rangle,

(752)

and the non-diagonal terms in the second sum vanish due to the zero mean of
X_{i}
.

We want to talk about (
727
) in this case, and the
\phi
function has its role in the discussion. If the function
\phi
satisfies the relation

\displaystyle X_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}=f(X_{i}),\quad\langle f(X_{i})\rangle=0

(753)

where
f(X_{i})
is an unknown function of
X_{i}
. Actually this relation leads to

\displaystyle t^{2}=\langle X_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}\rangle=0,

(754)

therefore in this case the distribution should be non-trivial with
\mu=0
and
t=0
. Usually we do not consider such cases, but we can still show how it works following this computation.
Then in this case the dominant terms in
\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle
will be (
751
) as it contains the largest number of terms. The
\phi
terms are not included since the averages of the non-diagonal
\phi
terms are zero because of (
731
) and (
746
). Then we can find that the dominant terms of
\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle
which locate at the order
p^{2}
or
N^{4}
cancel out in (
749
). Therefore the relation (
727
) is satisfied since the dominant terms in
Y^{2}
are also at the order
N^{4}
, so that the proposal holds in this case when
N
is sufficiently large.

While if
\phi
has the relation below like what we have in the Gaussian case

\displaystyle X_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}=g(X_{i}),\quad\langle g(X_{i})\rangle\neq 0

(755)

where
g(X_{i})
is also unknown and we denote the average with a nonzero constant
C
. Then the diagonal terms in the second sum can contribute to the dominant terms. We can have the explicit computation as the following,

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\left(X^{2}_{i}-\phi_{i}\right)X_{j}X_{k}\sim 2(N-2)C\sum_{j\neq k}X_{j}X_{k},

(756)

where the approximation is valid as we only care about the maximal numbers. In the square this term is similar to (
733
) except we have an extra parameter
4(N-2)^{2}
. The non-vanishing contribution after the average will be the diagonal terms in the square as
X_{i}
has a zero mean, which means the number of the terms is

\displaystyle 4(N-2)^{2}p=2N(N-1)(N-2)^{2}.

(757)

This contribution also locate at the order
N^{4}
which is additional comparing to the previous case, so that the dominant terms can not cancel out in (
749
). Therefore the proposal fails in this case.

After having discussed a simple example We can try to consider the general one

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}}X_{i_{1}}\dots X_{i_{q}},

(758)

where the sum contains
p
terms

\displaystyle p={N\choose q}

(759)

and
X_{i}
’s are still identical and independent variables. Following the procedure above we have

\displaystyle Y^{2}=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{q}}X_{i_{1}}^{2}\dots X_{i_{q}}^{2}+\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{q+1}}X_{i_{1}}^{2}\dots X_{i_{q-1}}^{2}X_{i_{q}}X_{i_{q+1}}+\dots+\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{2q}}X_{i_{1}}\dots X_{i_{2q}},

(760)

the numbers of the total terms for each sum are respectively

\displaystyle{N\choose q},\quad{N\choose q}{q\choose 1}{N-q\choose 1},\dots,{N\choose q}{N-q\choose q},

(761)

and the numbers for each single term in each sum or the repetitions are

\displaystyle 1,\quad{2\choose 1},\quad{4\choose 2},\dots,{2q\choose q}.

(762)

When
q=2
we can find that the computations in the two cases match.
For the function
\Phi
similarly we can define the functions

\displaystyle\Phi_{0},\quad\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{q},

(763)

which correspond to the terms in (
760
) respectively. And we can also introduce the
\phi
functions to express each
\Phi_{s}
, substituting the
X^{2}_{i}
by
\phi_{i}
in (
760
) we can get all the
\Phi_{s}
’s just like (
744
),(
745
).

Following the previous procedure the square of the error function can be evaluated as

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\left(Y^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}-\Phi\right)^{2}\rangle-\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2},

(764)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\sum_{s=0}^{q}\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{q+s}}\left(Y^{2}_{s}-\Phi_{s}\right)\right)^{2}\right\rangle-p^{2}\langle X^{2}\rangle^{2q},

(765)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{s=0}^{q}\left(\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{q+s}}\left(Y^{2}_{s}-\Phi_{s}\right)\right)^{2}\right\rangle-p^{2}\langle X^{2}\rangle^{2q},

(766)

where the index
s
labels the components in both
Y^{2}
,
\Phi
and the cross terms among the different sums vanish due to zero mean of
X_{i}
.

Then we’ll encounter the previous problem again that there are two different conditions (
753
),(
755
) for
\phi
, and we also first consider the former one. The total numbers of the diagonal terms in the above each sum
s
are respectively

\displaystyle{N\choose q},\quad{2\choose 1}{N\choose q}{q\choose 1}{N-q\choose 1},\dots,{2(q-1)\choose q-1}{N\choose q}{q\choose q-1}{N-q\choose q-1},

(767)

which are the combinations of (
761
) and (
762
) and the last one in (
760
) cancels out.
While about the non-diagonal terms as the averages of
X_{i}
is zero, the nonzero non-diagonal terms only appears in the sum
s=0
. And as the average of
\Phi_{i}
is also zero, the nonzero non-diagonal terms in the sum
s=0
will only come from
Y^{2}_{0}
. The computation of the square of this term is similar to (
760
) except the replacement of
X_{i}
by
X_{i}^{2}
,

\displaystyle Y^{4}_{nn}=\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{q+1}}X_{i_{1}}^{4}\dots X_{i_{q-1}}^{4}X_{i_{q}}^{2}X_{i_{q+1}}^{2}+\dots+\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{2q}}X_{i_{1}}^{2}\dots X_{i_{2q}}^{2},

(768)

where
nn
means nonzero non-diagonal. The non-zero contributions in (
766
) come from the square of the first sum
s=0
and the diagonal terms in the square of the other sums
s>0
. And note that the last one labelled by
s=q
cancels out, and in (
767
) the last one is subordinate to the square of the first term when
N
,
q
,
N/q
are sufficiently large. Then under the condition (
753
) the dominant terms will be the first sum with square according to the equation (
767
). The square of the first sum in (
766
) gives the dominant terms which are the last one in (
768
), it contains the number

\displaystyle{N\choose q}{N-q\choose q}.

(769)

Then after the average the dominant terms in the square of the error becomes

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=\left({N\choose q}{N-q\choose q}-{N\choose q}{N\choose q}\right)\langle X^{2}\rangle^{2q},

(770)

which is subordinate comparing to
\langle Y^{4}\rangle
.

While under the condition (
755
) the diagonal terms in the squares of the sums with
s>0
will contribute. The computation is similar to the previous case with
q=2
, the nonzero average increases the number of the diagonal or nonzero terms. As an example we calculate the last non-zero one in (
766
)

\displaystyle\sum_{ji_{1}\dots i_{2q-2}}(X_{j}^{2}-\phi_{j})X_{i_{1}}\dots X_{i_{2q-2}}=\alpha\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{2q-2}}X_{i_{1}}\dots X_{i_{2q-2}}

(771)

where

\displaystyle\alpha=C{N\choose q}{q\choose q-1}{N-q\choose q-1}/{N\choose 2q-2},

(772)

and for simplicity we denote the
\phi
term with
C
. Since we only care about the number of the terms, the denotation does not matter for the result.
The constant
C
is defined in (
755
) and the numerator is the number of the terms in the sum labelled by
s=q-1
, while the denominator is the number of the sum of the right hand side of (
771
). In the square of this term only the diagonal ones survive in the average, we have the number

\displaystyle{N\choose q}^{2}{q\choose q-1}^{2}{N-q\choose q-1}^{2}/{N\choose 2q-2}\sim N^{2q}\sim p^{2}.

(773)

Actually we can find that except the last sum all the sums in (
766
) will contribute to the dominant terms. So that the proposal will fail as the first sum cancels out with the last one in (
766
), while we have other additional contributions to the dominant terms.

CLT with
\mu\neq 0

In the above section we have discussed the case with
\mu=0
, after that we can move to consider the case with
\mu\neq 0
. Remember that for the distribution with a non-zero mean we deal it with the subtraction (
732
), now we keep this non-trivial mean. The difference between them may be illustrated by a simple example

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i\neq j}\left(X_{i}-\langle X_{i}\rangle\right)\left(X_{j}-\langle X_{j}\rangle\right),

(774)

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j}-\left\langle\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j}\right\rangle.

(775)

The two definitions are not equivalent which is manifest in the higher powers of
Y
. Actually the former one is equivalent to the case with
\mu=0
in the previous section, the latter without the subtraction is what we’ll consider in this section.

We still start with the simple case with
q=2

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}X_{j},

(776)

\displaystyle Y^{2}=\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}+2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}X^{2}_{i}X_{j}X_{k}+6\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq l}X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}X_{l}.

(777)

About
\Phi
we can have similar definition to (
735
), and the definition and the requirement for the error are the same as before. We also separate each sum in both
Y^{2}
and
\Phi
so that the square of the error can be expressed as

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\left(Y^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}-\Phi\right)^{2}\rangle-\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2},

(778)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(Y_{0}^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}_{0}-\Phi_{0}+Y_{1}^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}_{1}-\Phi_{1}\right)^{2}\right\rangle-\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2}.

(779)

And note that here we can not separate the square of the sum into the sum of the square of each sum, since now the cross terms are not zero due to the nonzero mean of
X_{i}
. When
N\to\infty
we can only consider the dominant terms, but as the case is different to before the terms we want will also be different. Now the dominant terms in the two parts in (
779
) are both in the second sum in (
777
) or
Y_{1}^{2}
(
779
), since this sum contains the largest number of terms and is non-zero under the averagre because of the non-zero mean.
Which means we can only consider the cross terms in the square of this sum which contains the largest number of terms. By the direct calculation we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}=2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\phi_{i}X_{j}X_{k},

(780)

where
\phi
is defined in (
743
). Then we have

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}_{1}-\Phi_{1}=2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\left(\left(X_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}\right)X_{j}X_{k}+\langle X_{i}\rangle^{2}\langle X_{j}\rangle\langle X_{k}\rangle\right),

(781)

while the dominant terms can be written as

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle_{d}=\left\langle\left(2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}\left(\left(X_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}\right)X_{j}X_{k}+\langle X_{i}\rangle^{2}\langle X_{j}\rangle\langle X_{k}\rangle\right)\right)^{2}\right\rangle-\left\langle 2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}X_{k}\right\rangle^{2}.

(782)

The dominant terms of the first part are the cross terms, whose number is at the same order of
N
as the second part. So to evaluate the approximation we can only consider the two terms below

\displaystyle\langle\left(X_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}\right)X_{j}X_{k}+\langle X_{i}\rangle^{2}\langle X_{j}\rangle\langle X_{k}\rangle\rangle,\quad\langle X_{i}^{2}X_{j}X_{k}\rangle,

(783)

where the variables are identical and independent. Recall that we have the equation (
746
)
therefore the two expressions are equal under the average. Explicitly the cross terms also contain many parts, we can also write down the one with the largest number of terms,

\displaystyle\left(X_{i}^{2}-\phi_{i}\right)X_{j}X_{k}\left(X_{l}^{2}-\phi_{l}\right)X_{m}X_{n},\quad 4{N\choose 2}(N-2){N-3\choose 2}(N-5),

(784)

while the second part in (
782
) contains

\displaystyle 4{N\choose 2}^{2}(N-2)^{2}.

(785)

Therefore the dominant contribution in (
782
) will cancel out, which makes the proposal hold.

We can also consider the problem that occurs in the case with
\mu=0
when the average of
X^{2}-\phi
is not zero, and we can find that there’s no such problem here. As the dominant term (
782
) contains the largest number of
X
terms, therefore even when
X^{2}-\phi
is a constant the total number of
X
terms in (
782
) will not change.

Next we consider the case with general
q
.
Following the previous procedure for the error we can get an expression similar to (
779
)

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=\left\langle\left(\sum_{s=0}^{q-1}(Y_{s}^{2}+\langle Y\rangle^{2}_{s}-\Phi_{s})\right)^{2}\right\rangle-\langle Y^{2}\rangle^{2},

(786)

and the dominant sum will also be the last non-zero one, i.e. the sum labelled with
s=q-1
.
To find out the behavior of the dominant terms the relation of the two functions below is important,

\displaystyle\langle\left(X_{i_{1}}^{2}-\phi_{i_{1}}\right)X_{i_{2}}\dots X_{i_{2q-1}}+\langle X_{i_{1}}\rangle^{2}\langle X_{i_{2}}\rangle\dots\langle X_{i_{2q-1}}\rangle\rangle,\quad\langle X_{i_{1}}^{2}X_{i_{2}}\dots X_{i_{2q-1}}\rangle,

(787)

we want to determine whether they are equal.
And we can find that the relation (
746
) still holds thus the approximation is also proper in general
q
.

SYK with one time point with
\mu=0

Like in the previous section given any distribution we can always construct a variable with zero mean, such as

\displaystyle\tilde{X}=X-\langle X\rangle,

(788)

so that we can consider any distribution we want.

We first consider a simple case with
q=2
, the Hamiltonian has the form

\displaystyle Y=\sum_{i\neq j}\text{sgn}(ij)X_{i}X_{j},

(789)

where
X_{i}
’s are identical and independent variables and the sign term mimics the sign function in the SYK model. Note that here we have no need to compute the explicit form of the sign function, since it has no effect yet. It is very similar to the previous case, but the difference is that here all
X_{i}
’s only appear once in the sum. Therefore for the square we have

\displaystyle Y^{2}=\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}+2\sum_{i\neq j\neq k\neq l}\text{sgn}(ijkl)X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}X_{l},

(790)

comparing to (
733
) it lacks the middle term. Our previous computation shows that the approximation fails in the Gaussian case with
\mu=0
due to the middle term, thus the approximation is proper in this case no matter what the distribution is.

We can have an explicit computation for the proposal, but first the convention needs to be consistent with the SYK model. Now the total number
N
counts the whole Majorana fermions and the number
q
is for the number of the fermions in the interaction. Then for
N/q=2
the expression (
789
) is still valid for the partition function, except that the
N
and
q
have different meanings. Then the number of
X_{i}
in (
789
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{\left(\left(N/2\right)!\right)^{2}},

(791)

and the sum contains the number of the terms

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{2!\left(\left(N/2\right)!\right)^{2}}.

(792)

Following the previous process we have

\displaystyle Y^{2}-\Phi=\sum_{i\neq j}\left(X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}-\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\right)

(793)

where
\phi
is defined in (
743
) and for the error

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle=\left\langle\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\left(X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}-\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\right)\right)^{2}\right\rangle-\left\langle\sum_{i\neq j}X_{i}^{2}X_{j}^{2}\right\rangle^{2}.

(794)

Since there are only two parts in the above function and the sums are the same, we can only compare the two expressions in the brackets. The average of
\phi
is zero due to the zero mean of
X_{i}
, so the proposal holds in the case. Explicitly the dominant terms in (
794
) come from the cross term in the first part and the whole second part, the number becomes

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{2!\left(\left(N/2\right)!\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{N!}{2!\left(\left(N/2\right)!\right)^{2}}-1\right)-\left(\frac{N!}{2!\left(\left(N/2\right)!\right)^{2}}\right)^{2}.

(795)

We can find the dominant terms cancel out so that the proposal is valid in this simple case.

Then we can consider a more general Hamiltonian with arbitrary
N
and
q
. Here we take the more familiar convention in SYK and still define
p=N/q
, then the partition function becomes

\displaystyle z=\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)J_{A_{1}}\dots J_{A_{p}}.

(796)

Before we check the approximation we can first have some computation about the numbers of the different terms in this partition function. The total number of the
J_{A}
’s is

\displaystyle{N\choose q},

(797)

and the number of the combinations of
J_{A}
’s is

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}},

(798)

while the times for a single
J_{A}
appearing in the Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle\frac{(N-q)!}{(p-1)!(q!)^{p-1}}.

(799)

The square of the partition function can be given as

\displaystyle z^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)^{2}J_{A_{1}}^{2}\dots J_{A_{p}}^{2}+\sum_{A,B}\text{sgn}(A)\text{sgn}(B)J_{A_{1}}^{2}\dots J_{A_{p-2}}^{2}J_{A_{p-1}}J_{A_{p}}J_{B_{p-1}}J_{B_{p}}

\displaystyle\quad+\dots+\sum_{A,B}\text{sgn}(A)\text{sgn}(B)J_{A_{1}}\dots J_{A_{p}}J_{B_{1}}\dots J_{B_{p}}

(800)

the first non-diagonal term appears with four different
J_{A}
’s as the combination
A
should be the permutation of
1,\dots,N
. And in the sums there may be many identical terms, here we may not give the explicit forms. To figure out the explicit expressions for the numbers in each sum we can count the numbers of different combinations of
r
different
J_{A}
’s in the product of the combinations of
A
and
B
. Obviously there’s only one combination in the case
r=1
, actually
r=1
is identical to
r=0
since the last
J_{A_{i}}
in a combination
A
can be determined by the other
p-1

J_{A_{i}}
’s. For the larger
r
we have to subtract the combinations in
r-i

(i<r)
to get the correct ones, as it can give
r-i
cases in the product of two combinations. Therefore we have

\displaystyle r=2,\quad\frac{(2q)!}{2!(q!)^{2}}-1,

(801)

\displaystyle r=3,\quad\frac{(3q)!}{3!(q!)^{3}}-{3\choose 2}\left(\frac{(2q)!}{2!(q!)^{2}}-1\right)-1,

(802)

from the above we can introduce the function
n_{r}
for this counting

\displaystyle n_{r}=\frac{(rq)!}{r!(q!)^{r}}-\sum_{s=2}^{r-1}{r\choose s}n_{s}-1.

(803)

From the calculation we can take mathematical induction to derive a simpler form

\displaystyle n_{r}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\frac{(sq)!}{s!(q!)^{s}}{r\choose s}(-1)^{r-s}+(-1)^{r},\quad r>1,

(804)

and

\displaystyle n_{0}=1,

(805)

where
n_{1}
is absent according to the equation (
800
).

Then we calculate the numbers of the terms in the each sum in (
800
),

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}n_{0},\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}{p\choose 2}n_{2},\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}{p\choose 3}n_{3},\dots,\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}n_{p},

(806)

and note that the first one is different to the others. And about the last one the dominant term of the subtrahend should be at the order
(N-q)!
, therefore the subtrahend is almost at the order
N(N-q)!
. Actually this number is far larger than the accurate one, since in (
804
) there’s a factor
(-1)^{r-s}
then the accurate number is approximately at
(N-q)!
.

Here we also have two conditions similar to (
753
),(
755
), and first we consider the former one. As in this section we assume the mean of the
J_{A_{i}}
is zero, therefore we have

\displaystyle\langle z^{2}\rangle=\left\langle\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)^{2}J_{A_{1}}^{2}\dots J_{A_{p}}^{2}\right\rangle=\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}\bar{J}^{2p}

(807)

where

\displaystyle\langle J_{A_{i}}^{2}\rangle=\bar{J}^{2}.

(808)

For the error we evaluate it as

\displaystyle\langle\text{Error}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\left(z^{2}-\Phi\right)^{2}\rangle-\langle z^{2}\rangle^{2},

(809)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{s=0,2}^{p}\left(\sum_{i_{1}\dots i_{q+s}}\left(z^{2}_{s}-\Phi_{s}\right)\right)^{2}\right\rangle-\langle z^{2}\rangle^{2},

(810)

where
s
does not take value
1
for clarity and the cross terms of different sums are zero due to the zero mean of
J_{A_{i}}
. Then we find that the computation is similar to before that for
s>0
the nonzero contribution under the average is the diagonal terms in the square, while for
s=0
the whole terms contribute to the computation of the error. Which means to find out the dominant sum
s
we should compare the square of the first term with the rest terms in (
806
), and note that the last one cancels out in (
810
) so that the non-trivial largest number should be the one labeled by
p-1
. And we give an upper bound for it

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}{p\choose p-1}n_{p-1}<p^{2}\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}\frac{(N-q)!}{(p-1)!(q!)^{p-1}}.

(811)

Note that the number of the identical terms in this sum may have an effect but we expect the upper bound is still valid. Then we can find that when
N
,
q
are sufficiently large it is subordinate to the square of the first term in (
806
). Therefore we write the dominant terms in (
810
)

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}n_{p}-\left(\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}\right)^{2}

(812)

where the cross terms of the first part and the whole second part contribute. Recall the expression (
804
) the dominant terms in the above equation cancel out, which implies the proposal holds.

Then we consider the second condition (
755
) that for a single variable we can view
J_{A_{i}}^{2}-\Phi_{i}
as a constant. The expression (
810
) is still valid so that the thing we need to consider is to find the correction to (
812
).
As an example we consider the behavior of the second sum labeled by
s=2
, the diagonal contribution after the subtraction should be

\displaystyle\alpha\sum J_{A_{p-1}}J_{A_{p}}J_{B_{p-1}}J_{B_{p}},

(813)

where
\alpha
is determined by the quotient of the two numbers of the two sum

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}{p\choose 2}n_{2}/\left({N\choose 2q}\frac{(2q)!}{2!(q!)^{2}}n_{2}\right).

(814)

Therefore after the square the total number becomes

\displaystyle\left(\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}{p\choose 2}n_{2}\right)^{2}/\left({N\choose 2q}\frac{(2q)!}{2!(q!)^{2}}n_{2}\right),

(815)

comparing to the two terms in (
812
) the above term is subordinate. Similarly we can find that after the subtraction the largest number in the sums
s>0
occurs in the case
s=p-1
, which gives

\displaystyle\left(\frac{N!}{p!(q!)^{p}}{p\choose p-1}n_{p-1}\right)^{2}/\left({N\choose N-q}\frac{(N-q)!}{(p-1)!(q!)^{p-1}}n_{p-1}\right).

(816)

The above contribution is still subordinate to the two terms in (
812
), which means the proposal is proper in this case.

Thus for the SYK model we can conclude that when the mean is zero the proposal works well no matter what the distribution is.

SYK with one time point with
\mu\neq 0

Like what we have discussed before the case with non-zero mean is different to last section. The whole computation is similar to the case with
\mu=0
except the mean terms, we can take relevant expressions from the previous sections. We consider the case with general
N/q
,

\displaystyle z=\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)J_{A_{1}}\dots J_{A_{p}},

(817)

the main task here is find the dominant terms.
The sign functions may have some effect which makes
s=q-1
not dominant, such as when
N/q
=2 we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)J_{A_{1}}J_{A_{2}}\right\rangle=\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)\langle J_{A_{i}}\rangle^{2}={N/2-1\choose[N/4]}\langle J_{A_{i}}\rangle^{2},

(818)

where the square bracket means the integer part. Actually here
N/4
is
q/2
, in the later computation we always have even
q
therefore we’ll omit the square bracket.

To derive this parameter explicitly we have two different ways, one is to find the sequence functions over different
N
which can be solved by Mathematica. Another is to consider different combinations, we can define the positive and the negative combinations then their difference gives the parameter. Given
N
different numbers we can have a permutation group which can be divided into the even and odd parts, and the two parts contain the same number of elements. If the indices of
J
have no order then the numbers of the even and odd parts are equal, so that the parameter is zero. But in the SYK model the indices are listed in a particular order, it makes the numbers of the two parts different.

To proceed the computation we define the sign of a
q
-sequence,

\displaystyle\text{sgn}(J_{A_{i}})=(-1)^{a_{i_{1}}+\dots+a_{i_{q}}},\quad q=0\text{mod}(4)

(819)

where when
q=2\text{mod}(4)
the sign will be opposite. Note that here we take the
a_{i}
from the indices of
J_{A_{i}}
, but what in the superscript should be the initial sites of the indices. When the indices take the values
1,\dots,N
, the values themselves naturally label their initial sites. But when the indices take arbitrary numbers or symbols, we can define an initial order and the sites with any permutation. After the assignment complete we can take the definition (
819
) to define the sign of any given
q
-sequence.

Then for a combination
A
containing
r
negative q-sequences we have the argument

\displaystyle\text{sgn}(A)=1,\quad\text{if }\sum_{i=1}^{r}s_{i}=0\text{mod}(2),

(820)

where
s_{i}
is the site of the
i
-th negative
q
-sequence.
We give the condition above as the negative
q
-sequences seems appearing in pairs. For
N/q=2
the positive combination
A
contains two positive q-sequences, so our mission is to find the first sequence with positive sign. As the combination has an order the first index in
J_{A_{1}}
is 1, we only need to consider the others. The positive
q
-sequence has the number

\displaystyle{N/2-1\choose 1}{N/2\choose q-2}+{N/2-1\choose 3}{N/2\choose q-4}+\dots+{N/2-1\choose q-1}{N/2\choose 0},

(821)

where
q=N/2
the first chooses odd number of odd integers while the second chooses even number of even integers. Then the negative
q
-sequence has

\displaystyle{N/2-1\choose 0}{N/2\choose q-1}+{N/2-1\choose 2}{N/2\choose q-3}+\dots+{N/2-1\choose q-2}{N/2\choose 1},

(822)

the difference of the two numbers will give the parameter

\displaystyle d(p,q)=\sum_{i=0}^{q-1}(-1)^{i+1}{pq/2-1\choose i}{pq/2\choose q-1-i},

(823)

which coincides with (
818
) when
N/q=2
. For general
p=N/q
we can also find an expression for finding the first positive
q
-sequence

\displaystyle d(p,q)=\frac{1}{p-1}{pq/2-1\choose q/2},

(824)

where
q
is even and it can be verified by numerics.

And note that for different
q
’s there may be a difference with the minus sign, which is explained in (
819
).

For general
N/q
we can try to compute
\gamma
in the expression below by the recursion

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)J_{A_{1}}\dots J_{A_{p}}\right\rangle=\sum_{A}\text{sgn}(A)\langle J_{A_{i}}\rangle^{p}=\gamma\langle J_{A_{i}}\rangle^{p}.

(825)

In the previous computation we have calculated the case with
p=2
, we only need to derive the
p+1
case from the
p
one. Given
N
numbers we can give the difference between the positive combinations and the negative ones which is denoted as
D(p,q)
and we have

\displaystyle D(2,q)=d(2,q),\quad D(p+1)=f(D(p,q)).

(826)

where
f
is an unknown function.
When we add extra
q
numbers to the
N
case which is already known, in the new permutation we can fix one number in the extra
q
ones at the
p+1
site in case of repetition. For simplicity we let the additional
q
numbers be
1,\dots,q
and put it at the first site, and we always fix the number
1
in this site.

We determine the first site through the procedure in the case of
p=2
, which can be divided into the positive and negative parts. After choosing
q
numbers from the
N+q
ones, about the left
N
numbers we assume we already know the difference between the positive combinations and the negative ones. To illustrate the product of the sequences, we define the positive (negative) sequences as
P_{+}
(
P_{-}
) so that we have

\displaystyle P_{+}P_{+}=P_{+},\quad P_{-}P_{-}=P_{+},\quad P_{+}P_{-}=P_{-}.

(827)

The product of two positive or two negative sequences gives a positive one while the product of one positive and one negative sequences gives a negative one. Then we can derive the sequences in the case
N+q
as

\displaystyle P_{+}^{N+q}=P^{q}_{+}P^{N}_{+}+P^{q}_{-}P^{N}_{-},\quad P_{-}^{N+q}=P^{q}_{+}P^{N}_{-}+P^{q}_{+}P^{N}_{-},

(828)

therefore the difference of the positive and negative sequences can be illustrated as

\displaystyle P_{+}^{N+q}-P_{-}^{N+q}=(P_{+}^{q}-P_{-}^{q})(P_{+}^{N}-P_{-}^{N}).

(829)

Which means the recursion can be expressed as

\displaystyle D(p,q)=d(p,q)D(p-1,q)=\prod_{s=2}^{p}d(s,q),

(830)

inserting the equation (
824
) it becomes

\displaystyle D(p,q)=\frac{(pq/2-1)!}{(p-1)!(q/2-1)!((q/2)!)^{p-1}}=\frac{(pq/2)!}{p!((q/2)!)^{p}}.

(831)

Then we can try to evaluate the cross terms in the square of the partition function (
800
). Consider the terms with
p-s
squares in the sequence,

\displaystyle\sum_{A,B}\text{sgn}(A)\text{sgn}(B)J_{A_{1}}^{2}\dots J_{A_{p-s}}^{2}J_{A_{p-s+1}}\dots J_{A_{p}}J_{B_{p-s+1}}\dots J_{B_{p}},

(832)

after the average over
J
the number in the sum becomes

\displaystyle d_{s}=\sum_{r=2}^{s}{p-r\choose s-r}{N\choose pq-rq}\frac{(pq-rq)!}{(p-r)!(q!)^{p-r}}D(r,q)^{2}(-1)^{s-r}+{p\choose s}(s-1)(-1)^{s-1}\frac{(pq)!}{p!(q!)^{p}},

(833)

where the derivation is similar to the equation (
804
).

We can find an upper bound for the number (
833
)

\displaystyle{N\choose pq-sq}\frac{(pq-sq)!}{(p-s)!(q!)^{p-s}}D(s,q)^{2},

(834)

Then for
s=2,\dots,p-1
we can compare the above number to the first one in (
806
), from this we may find out the dominant one.

By a few numerical comparisons it seems that the dominant terms do not locate at a particular
s
as the two parameters
p
,
q
vary. In this computation we can directly take the equation (
833
) rather than the one with approximation.

Appendix H
Some details about the fermion integral

The relevant integral is

\displaystyle I_{f}^{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{2N}\psi\exp\left(T[\sum_{i}[\Sigma_{LR}\psi_{i}^{L}\psi_{i}^{R}+\Sigma_{L}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{L}+\Sigma_{R}\theta_{i}\psi_{i}^{R}]\right)\,

(835)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int\text{d}\psi^{L}\text{d}\psi^{R}\exp(T\Sigma_{LR}\psi^{L}\psi^{R})\exp(T\Sigma_{L}\theta\psi^{L})\exp(T\Sigma_{R}\theta\psi^{R})\right)^{N}

(836)

Before evaluating this integral we need some specifications. Here
\psi^{L(R)}
are not Grassmann numbers but can be transferred into Dirac fermions as

\displaystyle c=\frac{\psi^{L}+\text{i}\psi^{R}}{2},\quad c^{\dagger}=\frac{\psi^{L}-\text{i}\psi^{R}}{2},\quad\psi_{L}=c+c^{\dagger},\quad\psi_{R}=\text{i}(c^{\dagger}-c)\,

(837)

\displaystyle\{c,c^{\dagger}\}=1,\quad\{c,c\}=\{c^{\dagger},c^{\dagger}\}=0\,.

(838)

Then the integral
I_{f}
becomes

\displaystyle I_{f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int\text{d}c\text{d}c^{\dagger}e^{a_{1}(cc^{\dagger}-c^{\dagger}c)+a_{-}c+a_{+}c^{\dagger}},\quad a_{1}=\text{i}T\Sigma_{LR},\quad a_{\pm}=T(\Sigma_{L}\pm\text{i}\Sigma_{R})\theta,

(839)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\cosh(\sqrt{a_{1}^{2}+a_{+}a_{-}})=4\cosh(T\sqrt{\Sigma_{L}^{2}+\Sigma_{R}^{2}-\Sigma_{LR}^{2}})

(840)

Next let us use the same method to compute (
382
):

\displaystyle I_{4}^{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\text{d}^{4N}\psi\exp\left(\Sigma_{ab}\psi_{i}^{a}\psi_{i}^{b}\right)

(842)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\int\text{d}^{4}\psi\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{12}\psi^{1}\psi^{2}+\Sigma_{13}\psi^{1}\psi^{3}+\Sigma_{14}\psi^{1}\psi^{4}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.\Sigma_{23}\psi^{2}\psi^{3}+\Sigma_{24}\psi^{2}\psi^{4}+\Sigma_{34}\psi^{3}\psi^{4}\right)\}^{N}.

Generally to compute
I_{4}
let us introduce two Dirac fermions

\displaystyle\psi_{1}=\sqrt{h}(c_{1}+c_{1}^{\dagger}),\quad\psi_{2}=\text{i}\sqrt{h}(c_{1}-c_{1}^{\dagger}),\quad\psi_{3}=\sqrt{h}(c_{2}+c_{2}^{\dagger}),\quad\psi_{4}=\text{i}\sqrt{h}(c_{2}-c_{2}^{\dagger}),

(843)

such that
\Sigma_{ab}\psi_{i}^{a}\psi_{i}^{b}
can be written as a
4\times 4
matrix
H
with non-vanishing elements

\displaystyle H_{11}=-\text{i}h(\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{34}),\quad H_{14}=-h(\Sigma_{13}-\Sigma_{24})-\text{i}h(\Sigma_{14}+\Sigma_{23})

(844)

\displaystyle H_{22}=\text{i}h(\Sigma_{34}-\Sigma_{12}),\quad H_{23}=-h(\Sigma_{13}+\Sigma_{24})+\text{i}h(\Sigma_{14}-\Sigma_{23})

(845)

\displaystyle H_{32}=h(\Sigma_{13}+\Sigma_{24})+\text{i}h(\Sigma_{14}-\Sigma_{23}),\quad H_{33}=-\text{i}h(\Sigma_{34}-\Sigma_{12})

(846)

\displaystyle H_{41}=h(\Sigma_{13}-\Sigma_{24})-\text{i}h(\Sigma_{14}+\Sigma_{23}),\quad H_{44}=\text{i}h(\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{34}).

(847)

By diagonalizing this matrix we can compute
I_{4}
as the trace of the exponential of the matrix

\displaystyle I_{4}=2\left(\cos\left(h\sqrt{(\Sigma_{14}-\Sigma_{23})^{2}+(\Sigma_{13}+\Sigma_{24})^{2}+(\Sigma_{12}-\Sigma_{34})^{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\cos\left(h\sqrt{(\Sigma_{14}+\Sigma_{23})^{2}+(\Sigma_{13}-\Sigma_{24})^{2}+(\Sigma_{12}+\Sigma_{34})^{2}}\right)\right).

(848)

And note that the change of the variable from
\psi
to
c
,
c^{\dagger}
will introduce an additional coefficient in the integral

\displaystyle(2h)^{2},

(849)

the final result is the product of the two terms.
